[00:19] the cyber kill chain or the cyber attack chain. This is actually a concept that came from the military and military exercises. The basic idea is this. Imagine there's a tank here and there's an enemy tank on the hill over there.
[00:34] This tank here that wants to kill that tank over there There's a whole series of steps that have to occur in order for this tank to kill the one over there. First of all, this tank has to be able to spot, see the tank over there. Then it has to be able to rotate its turret. And it has to be able to set its gun at the right elevation. I know nothing about tanks. Then it has to be able to load some ammunition, hit the fire button. The point is, there's a whole series of steps that need to occur in order for this tank to be able to kill the tank over there.
[01:03] And that's where the term kill chain idea comes from. The idea is that in order to achieve an objective, there's a whole chain of events that have to happen. And if I'm the tank over here on the hill, and I don't want to get shot and killed, then what I want to do is think about how can I break the chain? How can I make it so I'm not spotted by the enemy tank?
[01:22] How can I make it so that the tank can't rotate its turret or load ammunition or whatever? So it's a concept that came from the military. Lockheed Martin took this idea of this chain of events that have to occur that must to achieve some objective. Lockheed Martin took this objective and they applied it to cybersecurity. And of course, they called it the cyber kill chain or the cyber attack chain. And it's the exact same underlying idea, which is...
[01:49] If a hacker wants to break in and do nefarious things inside of your organization, there's a whole series, there's a whole chain of events that have to occur. And so what we wanna be doing as defenders is we wanna think about what are all the links in this chain? And how can we try and break one of those links? Because we can break a link in the chain, the attack can't proceed, and we're not going to get hacked. We're not going to get compromised, which is obviously desirable. So here's what you need to know. You need to know the seven links in this chain.
[02:18] You need to know the order of those links. And you need to know roughly what's happening at each link. So let me walk you through all of that in detail now. And we'll start high level with just the seven links in the chain. And then in a second here, I'll show you, or in a minute, I'll show you a detailed slide with detailed information on each of the seven steps. All right, so here's the first one. Step one in the Cyberkill chain is reconnaissance.
[02:40] Step two is weaponization. Three, there we go, is delivery. Four is exploit. Five is installation.
[02:53] Six is command and control. And seven is actions. So again, I'll show you a detailed slide and I'll walk you through what each of these steps mean in just a moment here. But just staying at the high level.
[03:04] remember you need to know each of the seven links in the chain you need to know the names of the links and you need to understand again what's the whole purpose of this this is a model that we can use as defenders to think about how can we try and protect our environment Because if we can try and break a link in the chain, for instance, if we can prevent some malicious software from exploiting our systems, not being able to install and run on our systems, then we can break this link in the chain. And if we can break this link in the chain, the attack's not going to be able to proceed any further. The attacker's not going to be able to get into our environment and perform nefarious actions. So that's the whole purpose of this model. Think about how we as the defenders can break each link in the chain.
[03:46] And for the exploit, for instance, here, Typically, or one of the major ways that we can try and prevent malware from being able to exploit our systems is by patching our systems. So if we can patch our systems as quickly as possible in a timely manner, then we can try and break this link in the chain. And of course, the other thing we wanna be thinking about this is we wanna try and break the link as early as possible it's probably not possible for you to entirely prevent the attackers from knowing you even exist but you know if you could that'd be great so and of course you know because the earlier you can break the link in the chain the less far the attackers are going to get into their attack, and that's obviously desirable. So that's the whole idea here. And here's the details. Here's the seven links in the Cyberkill chain. Step one, as I said, or link one is reconnaissance.
[04:32] And this is where the attacker is researching the target organization. And specifically, one of the main things they're trying to do is figure out what systems are running in this environment and what are potential vulnerabilities in those systems that could be exploited. So this is happening during the reconnaissance. This is what's happening during reconnaissance.
[04:49] The attacker is reconnoitering, figuring out what systems are running, what vulnerabilities exist in the target environment. Step two, weaponization. This is where the attacker is creating some custom malware that will be able to exploit the vulnerabilities that have been identified. Step three, delivery. This is where the attacker is trying to think about how can they get the malware into the target environment.
[05:15] Can they get it in through a spear phishing email or a whaling email or smishing? Or maybe they could do a baiting attack by sprinkling USB sticks through the company employee parking lot and having an employee pick one up and walk that USB stick through all the layers of physical security and plug into their system. That's the point of delivery. The attacker is trying to figure out how can they get the malware into the target environment. Step four, exploit. This is where the malware is actually going to be triggered and will run in the environment, exploit some vulnerability in the system. Step five, installation. As soon as the attacker has managed to get a foothold in the target environment, they're going to want to make sure they can maintain that persistence. And that's typically by installing some sort of backdoor, which is going to make it so that even if the company patches their systems, the attackers still have a way in. They can maintain that persistence.
[06:09] which is then going to allow them to maintain a persistent connection and start to send command and controls into the target environment, which will then, of course, allow them to perform nefarious actions, ransomware attacks, exfiltrate sensitive data, perform denial of service attacks, whatever they want to do. So... One final reminder, know all seven links in the chain, know the order of them, and know roughly what's happening each link. And the whole point of this is we as defenders should be thinking through how can we break each link in this chain and how can we break the chain as early as possible to better defend our systems.

[00:03] 🎵 🎵
[00:13] So we've defined what the word model actually means. A model is simply a representation of something. And from a security perspective, what we have are security models that represent what security needs to look like in architectures. they present the rules by which we can implement to achieve either confidentiality, integrity, and possibly some other requirements from a security perspective.
[00:44] That's what security models provide for us. a representation of what something needs to look like the rules that need to be implemented in order to achieve security but basically a model is a representation of something and because it is a conceptual representation of something we need to implement that model to actually make it work. But basically what we do to protect any architecture, and you know this, we take the entire architecture which is made up of different components, and we break it down into the individual components that represent that entire architecture and we protect each one and the way that we decide how to protect each of those components is always going to be based on value so the Diagram that you see here obviously represents a camera, digital camera. But if you want to protect that entire architecture, you're going to have to break it down into components, protect each one. Now, here's something that you need to remember, however, would it be easier to take something that already exists and then break it down into components and protect each one? Or would it be easier to design the security and build the security into each of the components as you're building it from scratch by going through the engineering process?
[02:10] And the obvious answer to that is it's always easier to design and build security as you're building the entire architecture rather than to add it later. Always bake security into whatever architecture you need to protect by building the right level of security into each of the components that make up the entire architecture. itself that's the most efficient cost-effective now here's the problem that we need to remember each of the components may be made up of sub components and then those sub components may be made up of sub sub components To what level do you go to? And here's an example.
[02:53] The hardware component may be made up of firmware and a processor and a hard drive. And to what level do you go to will always be dictated by the value that you're trying to protect. That's nothing new for you, but that's what dictates how we protect architectures and how security models will guide us into what that representation looks like and how security is added into each of the components to ultimately protect the entire architecture itself.

## Video 6 — Information Flow Models & Covert Channels
- Duration: 13:33
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/99e6be6b-5aee-493f-b423-5e781e3fc42e
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Information Flow Model 00:40; Clark-Wilson Model 06:28; Clark-Wilson: 3 Goals of Integrity 06:55; Clark-Wilson: 3 Rules 08:25; Brewer-Nash (The Chinese Wall) Model 10:36; Graham–Denning Model 12:01; Harrison–Ruzzo–Ullman Model 12:37
- Transcript: 00:14–13:18; 25 blocks / 208 phrase lines (stable across five loads; the panel repeats the covert-channel definition line at 01:31 and again inside the 01:48 block — preserved in both places)

[00:14] All right, so far we've been talking about security models and just as a reminder, there are two types of models that exist, lattice-based and rule-based. And so in this section, we're gonna talk about some of the rule-based models that are important for us to focus on. And we are gonna start with, again, some of those models that are referred to as rule-based. And so the first one that we need to focus on is something called the information flow model.
[00:44] And if you look at those words, it tells you exactly what this model actually does. Because if we can track the flow of information from the time that it's created or acquired, to the time that it gets destroyed at some point in the future. If you can track that life cycle from start to finish, you may be able to identify where certain insecurities and vulnerabilities may exist, including what we call covert channels. Covert channels, very important for us to focus on covert channels from a security perspective.
[01:20] Now the word covert channel is defined as, and here is the key word that you always need to look for. It's a unintentional communications path that has the opportunity of disclosing confidential information. So the word unintentional tells us exactly what this is. We don't mean it for it to be there, but it's there and because it's there, it has the opportunity of disclosing confidential information.
[01:48] And so this exists in all kinds of architectures, especially in technology. Now we can, and we'll show you some examples of this, but for now, just remember the definition of what a covert channel is. Unintentional communications path, that has the opportunity of disclosing confidential information. There are only two types, only two types of covert channels, storage and timing.
[02:17] And the difference between them is pretty fundamental. If you can exploit the storage capabilities of a system into unintentionally giving away confidential information, that would be an example of a storage covert channel. If you can exploit the timing capabilities of a unintentional communications path, then that would be referred to as a timing covert channel. Now in technology, which is what we're mostly focused on from a security perspective in organizations, it's very difficult, in fact, impossible to find an example of a timing covert channel.
[02:58] 99.999% of all covert channels that exist in technology end up being storage and in fact a very simple example of that would be what happens in most architectures like your laptop for example or any computer for that matter. Let's say a process writes information to storage and then leaves it there. And that information is very sensitive. The next process that comes along that has access to RAM may be able to read that information.
[03:28] So even though we don't mean for that information to be disclosed, to further processes. Just the way that architecture operates, that information was left there. Most architectures don't have the ability to overwrite, for example, anything sensitive that has been written to volatile memory, and so that makes it available to further processes. That's a pretty good example of a storage cover channel.
[03:51] Timing cover channels are very difficult to find but one that was made very famous many years ago was just before the start. of the first Gulf War, which I think was referred to as Desert Storm. But somebody kept track of how many pizzas were being ordered and delivered to the Pentagon. in Washington, for example, and they knew that on any given day, for example, or evening, there are three or four pizzas being delivered to the Pentagon. That's normal, that's usual.
[04:24] But just before the first Gulf War happened, there were like 30 or 40 pizzas being delivered to the Pentagon in the evening. So if you notice this, you can probably infer and deduce some sensitive information, like there's a lot more people working at night at the Pentagon. What do they do there? Well, besides other things, they plan military actions.
[04:47] So I bet you they're planning military action against Iraq because dialogue between those two countries has degraded to the point where a conflict might start. And I think it was one of the media outlets that were able to determine that. And they knew almost to the hour when that military action was going to commence, simply because they kept track of pizzas being delivered to the Pentagon. And so that is definitely an example of a timing covert channel.
[05:15] Unintentional, but it did disclose very confidential information. Here's another example that I think the media has made a real big deal of and you may not recognize what you see on the slide here but this happened a little while ago but it disclose the locations of army bases around the world that belong to the U.S. because the soldiers that were stationed at these army bases were using their Fitbits and running, for example, not only around the army bases, but also within. And then that information was actually uploaded to certain websites.
[05:58] And if you correlated with Google Earth, then you'd be able to obviously understand exactly where these army bases were around the world. So that's an example of a unintentional communications path that again did disclose very confidential information. That's probably one that you know of. Now, continuing on with different security models, and again the word model means it's a representation of something.
[06:27] So the next model that is significant for us to understand from an exam perspective is what we call the Clark-Wilson model, the Clark-Wilson model. This is also rule based. And what you want to remember about this one is that it also only addresses integrity. So it's a integrity only model, but it has an advantage over the other integrity model that we've already talked about, which is Biba. Whereas Biba, if you look at the slide here, There are three goals of integrity and they are listed at the top of this slide here.
[07:01] The three goals of integrity are preventing unauthorized people from making any modifications. That's the only goal that Biba can actually address. And so when these guys came around, Clark and Wilson, they decided to create a model that could address all three goals of integrity that you see there. not only preventing unauthorized people from making any modifications, but if you really want to address integrity, you also have to prevent authorized people from making improper modifications.
[07:34] The Biba model cannot address that. But the Clark-Wilson model does, and it also addresses the third goal of integrity, which as you can see here, is maintaining consistency of the entire environment itself. Because if you can't trust the integrity of the components, that make up the entire architecture, then you really can't trust the accuracy and the integrity of the data, for example, that ultimately is stored within that architecture. And so what you need to remember here are the three goals of integrity and they are preventing unauthorized people from making any modifications, preventing authorized people from making improper modifications, and maintaining consistency of the entire system itself.
[08:20] And the way that the Clark-Wilson model does it and this is what's important for you to remember, is by implementing those three simple rules that you see at the bottom of this slide here. And the three simple rules are well-formed transactions, separation of duties, and what we call the access triple. And very briefly, what those mean is the first one, well-formed transaction means that we only apply good transactions to the architecture. Let's say we're using a database.
[08:51] If you want to maintain the integrity of the content of the database itself, you've got to make sure that you only apply good, well-formed transactions. That's what that means. The second rule is that you need to inform or you need to enforce separation of duties, which means that certain tasks within that database environment, if we use that as an example, should not be done by the same individual alone. Certain tasks that may affect the integrity of the architecture should be separated so that they are performed by different individuals to ensure more integrity.
[09:29] Separation of duties, you know exactly what that means. And the third one is called the access triple. And if you think about those two words, it tells us exactly what it means. In order to allow a subject to access an object like that database, you cannot give that access directly.
[09:48] It has to go through a third component that is in the middle. That's why we call this the access triple. There are three components to that access subject to program. to object.
[10:02] And so because it's the program that it's in between, that program can enforce, for example, certain security rules that would ultimately ensure the integrity of the information that's stored within that architecture or database. So what you need to remember as a summary are the three goals of integrity and how the Clark-Wilson model addresses all three by implementing three simple rules. Those three simple rules are well-formed transactions, separation of duties and the access triple. If you remember that you'll be just fine. Now there is another model that we need to talk about and it's referred to as the Brewer Nash model and it's also referred to as the Chinese wall model. And the reason it's called the Chinese wall model is because we put up a wall in between environments to address what this model only addresses, and that is conflict of interest.
[11:00] And so this model is only interested in preventing, for example, conflict of interest situations. And so the idea is, for example, if you have one person that has been able to access, for example, the development environment, that same person should not be able to access the production environment because knowledge of both would be a conflict of interest. And so the idea is the system that has been implemented using this model would recognize that that person has had access to development and so therefore should not have access to production. The idea is, if that person had been able to access production first, the system would allow it, but then if she tried to access the development environment, that would not be allowed.
[11:45] We're trying to prevent conflict of interest situations, but obviously in today's environments, is very important to most organizations. And two other really simple models that we need to talk about, and we can go through these very quickly, but there is another model called Graham Denning. And all you need to remember about this is that it specifies very specific rules between subjects and objects. So specific rules between subjects and objects is what this model provides.
[12:17] So for example, user Joe has read access to the payroll databases. That would be an implementation of this model because this model provides a way to have specific rules between subjects and objects. That's all you need to remember about that. Now there is a variation or enhancement of this model called the Harrison-Ruzzo-Allman result or model.
[12:42] And this is basically the Graham-Denning model that we just talked about where you have specific rules between subjects and objects. But what this model adds is simply generic rights. Look for that word. Generic rights is what this model adds. And what do we mean by generic rights? Simply rights that are applied to everyone universally.
[13:04] So for example, we could add read capabilities to everyone. plus have specific rules between subjects and objects. And that would be an implementation of the Harrison-Russell-Allman model, which adds generic rights to really the Graham-Denning model.

Is that that's true and so it'd be nice if there was some sort of objective measurement system. that would provide us objective and independent measurements for example of the security capabilities of the products that we can buy from vendors. and that's what we call evaluation criteria so if you look at this slide, you'll see what are the evaluation criteria that exists today because Every vendor out there wants to be able to say,
[01:49] Whether they're thinking it's the best or they bought them to make, is something that we can measure, and understand fully. And so a measurement system that can do that is what we're talking about, and their capabilities and evaluate those capabilities, So these measurement systems have been around for a very long time and there are three significant systems that each of them in turn, that can provide these measurements and we're going to look at each one of those. And the very first one is called the Orange Book.

Before we get into these measurement systems, which we call the ITSEC, we need to understand the concepts of certification and accreditation.

For example, if we have to buy a firewall to protect our network from the internet, the internet the obvious solution is go out and find a firewall and implement it, but there could be several firewalls that we've decided to look at and we want to which one would be best for our organization, we would go ahead and take a look at the different firewalls that we've decided to look at and to certify that each one of them would work in our environment.

We are certifying, we're doing a comprehensive technical analysis to understand of each of those firewalls and ensure that it meets our needs and we decide for example for example that three of them would work in our environment so we've certified three firewalls, to implement all three firewalls in our environment, so we need to pick one, and it's probably going to be the most cost effective one but that is what accreditation is Accreditation as you see here is really going to be the official management decision to be the official management decision to accept the risk of and implement one of those solutions and accreditation is typically done by management and is not done by security. management would decide that we're going to use this firewall and accept the risk of using this firewall. At the end of which, we're going to have to go through the entire certification and accreditation process again. Now let's get into these three measurement systems that have been built to evaluate products. The very first one is called the Orange Book.
[05:48] And you can see why it's called the Orange Book. Its cover is actually orange. And it's part of what we call the Rainbow Series, a series of documents that have been put out by the Department of Defense for many years. Each one of these documents in the Rainbow Series has its own colour, have something to do with security.
[06:10] For example, there's a light blue book that has to do with network security. There's a red book that has to do with network security in the traditional sense. But the orange one or the one that has the orange cover is the one or being able to measure the security capabilities of products and is exactly what we're talking about. of the things that we have to remember about all of these measurement systems or these systems, evaluation criteria systems, is that they need to be objective and independent measurements. It wouldn't do us any good if we allowed the vendors themselves to evaluate their own products. and so the way that this works is let's say we are a vendor that wants to sell our product and we've developed our product and we're ready to market it and sell it we would hire a for example orange book licensed organization to come and and rate our product and if it gets a good rating then we can sell it as a good product not because we the vendor are saying it's a good product but because the independent and objective measurement system says it's a good product.
[07:25] So these systems are really good for us as consumers because we can trust the ratings that have been given to these products because they've been given by independent objective organizations. But it's also good for the vendors themselves because if they want to market their products they can compare them to other products and identify the features they don't have and so they want to probably have the same features and capabilities in their products that other products have from competitors now the orange book is something that was developed back in the mid 80s and if you look at what architectures in the mid 80s looked like compared to today, They look much different than they do today and because of that, the Orange Book is obsolete, but it really isn't from one perspective. First of all, the Orange Book only measures confidentiality.

They're obviously an organization that is particularly concerned about confidentiality. So the one thing you need to remember about the Orange Book is that it only measures confidentiality.
[08:44] And the other problem with the Orange Book is that because of what architectures looked like back in the mid 80s, networks weren't what they are today. And so this system does not work well for products that is meant to run in a network. It's really meant for standalone type of box architectures that are that are not meant for a network. Now when you look at the classifications the Orange Book uses, that the Orange Book uses, if you look at this slide, and we basically start at the bottom And as we go up we get more secure.
[09:24] So a product for example that is A1 capable has the most confidentiality. In fact I don't think anything has ever received a rating of A1 because that system would obviously be the ultimate in terms of having confidentiality features from a confidentiality perspective. Most firewalls, for example, end up being at the B1 level.
[09:48] Most operating systems end up being at the C2 level. And what you need to remember here is that each of the levels will inherit all the security requirements of the levels below it. So for example, if we go to B1, We have everything that's included in C2 plus they add what's listed there under B1 which is security labels. So B1 would include everything from C level and below, and now it's one level that for example we need to remember,
[10:26] at what level of the Orange Book is labeling first a requirement? And the answer is, as you can see there, B1. And as you keep going up, we have more security requirements to implement at each level. But basically this is what the Orange Book looks like.
[10:47] Now if you remember the two problems with the Orange Book. First of all it only measures confidentiality. And secondly, it's only meant for single box type architectures that don't work well in a network environment. The Europeans came along and said, wow, this is a great idea.
[11:07] but as usual you North Americans did a lousy job of implementing it and we'll try to take it and improve it. And that's basically what the ITSec is. Think of the ITSec as the orange book with those problems that the Orange Book has addressed. In other words, this not only measures confidentiality, but it can also measure integrity and availability requirements.
[11:33] And this, the ITsec, works well in a network environment. And what you need to remember about the ITsec is that one of the other improvements that they made is that there are two measurements that are given according to the ITsec. One for functional requirements. And one for assurance requirements.
[11:56] Because if you remember back to what we discussed as being good characteristics of a control or any control really needs to apply functionality really well, but also the assurance that it's making the right decisions. And I think the example that illustrates this very well would be a firewall. The functional requirements of a firewall is that it needs to be able to allow or block traffic. And that is the functional pillar, the functional requirement.
[12:25] But what assurance do we have that that firewall is actually making the right decisions on a regular basis is the assurance part of what we're talking about here that firewall. So those two pillars need to be addressed. The other improvement that the Europeans made to, so that these two measurements, or evaluation criteria is that they said let's look at products and give them two ratings, one for functionality and one for assurance because you could have products that do a really good job functionally but don't give you so much assurance or vice versa and so it'd be nice to have two ratings for each of those. Now what you need to remember is that the ITSec uses the same classification levels for functionality as the Orange Book, just saw in the previous slide, for example, the classification levels that apply to the Orange Book.
[13:21] are the ones that the ITSec also uses for functionality, so we go from, E is where there isn't much security and then we get into the C level C1 C2 and then B1 B2 B3 and A1
[13:35] All right, so those are the functional ratings that we're talking about here. But then there's also E-level assurance ratings that they created as part of this and what you see on the next slide here are the e-ratings now you don't need to memorize these but all you need to know is that we go from E0 which means that that product does not provide very good assurance all the way up to E6 which means that product does give good assurance capability and so as we go up we increase the ability of a product to provide assurance.

## Video 8 — Evaluation Criteria: Common Criteria
- Duration: 10:10 (schedule; the page header/player fluctuated between 10:09 and 10:10 across loads)
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/fd8cf082-018e-4bfb-9c48-3e31dd2ffe2d
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Evaluation Criteria 00:19; Common Criteria 01:07; Common Criteria Process 02:33; Common Criteria EAL levels 08:09
- Transcript: 00:14–09:52; 14 blocks / 150 phrase lines (stable across three loads)

[00:14] The evaluation criteria systems we've talked about, they're good, And if you remember back to the Orange Book and the ITSEC, they had a couple of problems. One of the problems had to do with the fact that the Orange Book only measures confidentiality and ITSEC only measured confidentiality, integrity and availability. But as you know, security is much more than just confidentiality, integrity and availability. Just think of all of the requirements that we have for security. So for example, a business continuity plan, a disaster recovery system, be able to go through an audit, an independent audit and demonstrate that their systems are secure. All of these things are what we want to be able to measure. But the Orange Book and ITSEC, those systems couldn't measure them. And so we need a system that could measure the whole spectrum of security. And that's what Common Criteria is.
[01:16] That's what Common Criteria does. It measures the whole spectrum of security. So Common Criteria Unlike the other systems that we've talked about before that were developed by the Department of Defense etc. this system is a global system. So it's created by a whole consortium of countries around the world and it's a single system that has been adopted by the ISO the International Organization for Standardization. And so it's also sometimes called ISO 15408. But when we think about the Common Criteria, it is a system that measures the whole spectrum of security. Probably the other big thing to know about the Common Criteria, is that, if you look at this slide, we've talked about evaluation systems, but we haven't talked about a process. And Common Criteria goes much further than all of the other systems do, because it has a process that is built into it. And a lot of exam questions will focus on that process. So for example, the process starts off with the Protection Profile.
[02:29] So the protection profile is a list of requirements that a product has to meet. Now those requirements will be determined by the user community. So for example, if we're a bunch of users who want to buy a firewall, all of us would get together and collectively decide what are the security requirements that our firewall has to meet. And as you can see here, they could be specific functions or non-specific functions, or they could be functions relating to control or assurance requirements. It's a little bit of all of them.
[02:55] The next step in the process of the Common Criteria is what we call an ST. It's a security target. And the security target is also a list of requirements that the product has to meet. The ST is written by the vendor of the product. And so if we go back to that firewall example, the vendor would write a security target that says, "Here are the security requirements that our firewall has to meet, and it's what we claim our firewall is." So the ST is the vendor's statement of the security requirements that their product meets. The ST is a product specific thing.
[03:43] And then once the ST has been written, the next step in the process is the TOE. The Target of Evaluation. The TOE is simply the product that is being evaluated. It goes without saying, that the TOE is the product.
[04:01] And then the TOE will be evaluated by an evaluation team. That evaluation team is an independent lab and it evaluates the TOE based on the Protection Profile, the security target that was written by the vendor.
[04:21] So the TOE will be evaluated. And as you can imagine, it's impossible for an evaluation lab to be a specialist in every single product, because the products are so diverse. And so what the Common Criteria does is it has a process where the evaluation lab creates a set of guidelines or a set of procedures for evaluating that specific product. And so the evaluation lab will create a set of procedures for evaluating a firewall, for example. And then it will evaluate the firewall according to those procedures.
[05:02] And what's the result of that evaluation? It's the EAL, the Evaluation Assurance Level. So whatever EAL that is achieved by a product is the result of that evaluation. And at this point it is important to know that the EAL is a product's assurance level. The EAL is not a measure of a product's security. The EAL is not a function, it's an assurance rating.
[05:30] For example, the EAL can be anywhere from 1 to 7. And there are examples on the other slide that you'll see shortly, which is one of the key things that you need to remember here about Common Criteria. The evaluation assurance level is achieved by a product by the EAL. Actually, wait a minute.
[05:54] I need to clarify. Do you see the EAL? Is really just a measure of the assurance of the product. The way to think about the EAL, does that make sense? But what about the functional requirements that surround the product? How do we measure those functional requirements? All we can do is evaluate the EAL for assurance, but those functional requirements are not necessarily met, and as you can see here, the number next to the functional requirements is always paired with the EAL. It says that if you're going to be EAL3, you have to have, for example, this number of functional requirements or because, and as you can see here, that number always goes up as the EAL goes up. So as the EAL goes up, the functional requirements go up.
[06:45] And one last thing that you need to remember about the EAL, is there are up to 7 EAL levels. But EAL1 to EAL4 are not verified by the government, they are verified commercially. EAL5 to EAL7 have to be verified. EAL5 to EAL7 are for government systems, and they're verified by the government. And so EAL1 through EAL4 can be done by any lab. EAL5 to EAL 7 has to be done by a government lab. That's the difference.
[07:27] Now there are a couple of other things to remember about the Common Criteria, because this system does measure the whole spectrum of security. You need to have a process that if it is to be used, you might have to go through a very long process to get certified. And it is important to remember this is a very robust system, it is not something that you can just go and do overnight. It takes a while to do. It consumes a lot of time and money to get something certified, so that's one of the reasons that so many vendors follow it. It is tough, it is costly, and it is time consuming.
[08:15] And finally, the EAL's. In this course, you're going to need to know exactly what the EAL's are. And so this is the slide. As you can see here, there's EAL1 which is functionally tested. EAL2 is structurally tested. EAL3 is methodically tested and checked. EAL4 is methodically designed, tested, and reviewed. EAL5 is semiformally designed and tested. EAL6 is semiformally verified design and tested. EAL7 is formally verified design and tested. So EAL7 is the highest level that is available and is formally verified design and tested. This is very important to an evaluator. And that level is like as secure as you're going to get because it has been designed with the highest possible level of security. So if you are trying to select a product that has the highest EAL level, you would select EAL7. But it is important for you to consider that not every vendor would want to be or could be EAL7 certified, because it would be very costly for them to do so. And so, the cost benefit analysis of having a product that is EAL7 certified might not justify the cost, so you must choose the appropriate assurance level for your office. And that is about as much as I can teach you about Common Criteria.
[09:42] I hope you've found these two videos about evaluation criteria to be useful. Because We've just taken a look at the three evaluation systems for measuring products. And they're called the Orange Book, ITSEC, and the Common Criteria and it's all about the different levels that are part of the common criteria.

So you could sue Enron, which went bankrupt, but you couldn't make these individuals accountable, really. And that, frankly, pissed a lot of people off. So the US Congress acted.

And I'm way oversimplifying this here, but Basically, one of the provisions of SOX requires that the senior executives of publicly traded companies in the US, it requires these senior executives, like the CEO, the CFO, to sign off on the financial results that are being produced. And if it is found, that those senior executives willfully, intentionally, were committing fraud. Then, and here's the key piece, they could be personally liable. And this was a tectonic shift in the way that liability worked in the US. And this obviously freaked a lot of senior executives out.
[09:07] They're like, wait, by signing off on this, if there's like problems in this and, you know, if I'm found out that I was committing fraud, I can be personally liable. You can take away all my millions of or billions or whatever I'm being paid. That freaked a lot of them out. And That's actually why we're talking about it here, because Sarbanes-Oxley has really helped to promote the tone from the top that we want, where we want senior executives invested in security.
[09:30] We want them to care about security. And Sox really helped about that because of course, senior executives realize the numbers that they have to sign off on, those numbers are coming out of computer systems. So those computer systems better have confidentiality, integrity, et cetera. And so SOC's not a security control framework, but it has helped to promote this tone from the top to ensure that senior executives are actually invested in having good security controls within their organization.
[09:53] So it's kind of an interesting one. FedRAMP. This one's easy to remember. Any US federal government agency, Fed, any US federal government agency that wants to use cloud services, those cloud services have to be FedRAMP compliant.
[10:10] So FedRAMP is a security control framework specific to cloud security controls. And it's actually even a whole certification framework as well attached to it. But The simple thing you have to remember for the exam is that FedRAMP is a security control framework specific to cloud security controls. And any US federal government agency that wants to use cloud services, those cloud services have to be FedRAMP compliant. And then the final one here, FISMA. This is, again, another U.S.
[10:37] government agency standard. And it's a requirement that U.S. government agencies are in compliance with FSMA. It's another security control framework. And it's actually a requirement for some U.S. government agencies. You don't need to know which ones. But it's a requirement for some U.S. government agencies to be compliant with FSMA. So it's just another security control framework. OK, so that's the ones that you need to know just a tiny bit about.
[11:01] There is one security control framework that you need to know a bit more about, and that is ISO 27001. And the reason you need to know more about ISO is that if you were to survey the planet asking, and trying to figure out what is the most commonly used security control framework, I think you'd find the answer is this, ISO 27001. Less so here in Canada and in the US. It's still used very commonly, but not quite as commonly. Europe, other parts of the world, it's much more pervasively used. So that's why we're going to talk about it in a bit more detail because it is such a commonly used security control framework. So first of all, here is the official definition.
[11:39] ISO 27001 provides best practice recommendations for an ISMS. What the heck is an ISMS? An Information Security Management System. What is that? It's basically this. It's a security function.
[11:55] So what ISO 27001 provides is the best practice recommendations for a security department, a security function. So if you were to think about it, what would you expect to have in place in a well-run security function? Well, it would start with having the right governance in place to ensure that the security function was aligned with overall corporate goals and objectives. So making sure the security function had the right policies, procedures, et cetera. In a well-run security function, you then want to make sure that there's a good onboarding process, a good access management process, a good asset management process, configuration and change control processes, network security controls.
[12:32] backup and recovery controls, cryptographic controls, a compliance function, etc. So if you were to step back and think about what would a well-run security function look like, that's what ISO 27001 provides. That's what an ISMS is. An information security management system is a security function.
[12:51] There's a companion document to ISO 27001 that you need to know about, which is ISO 27002. So here's the relationship. ISO 27001 is a much shorter document because it basically just covers the controls. ISO 27002 is a much longer document, because what ISO 27002 provides is all of the implementation guidance for the controls in 27001. Let's say you look at a control in 27001, and you're like, how would I actually implement multi-factor authentication?
[13:22] You could look at 27002 and you would find that there's a ton of detail in there about here's exactly how you would do that. So 27002 provides a bunch of detailed implementation guidance for how to implement the controls in 27001.
[13:37] And that leads to a good exam question. Which of these could an organization be certified against? And the answer is 27001. The reason being, an auditor can come in and audit an organization against a control framework, against a set of controls. You can't be audited against a guidance document. And actually pay attention here. This is actually very common.
[14:03] Again, not so much here in Canada and the US, but certainly in Europe and other parts of the world. It's very common for service providers to be ISO 27001 audited and certified. And service providers use that as a means of demonstrating that they have good security controls in place. If a customer is going to a service provider and saying, hey, Do you have good security controls? The service provider can hand them their ISO 27001 certification and say, yeah, we do. We passed.
[14:27] We've been ISO 27001 certified. That indicates that we have good security controls in place. So remember that. You can be certified. An organization can be certified against ISO 27001.
[14:39] One more little tiny tidbit here related to ISO 27001. If you were to ask the question, where in the ISO 27001 document are the controls actually listed out? The answer is in Annex A, Appendix A. So if you were to look in the ISO 27001 document, where you would find all of the, there's like, these are the 14 domains and there's about 140 odd controls.
[15:01] If you were to look where they actually list it out, it's in Annex A. By the way, I would not suggest you need to memorize all 14 domains here, and you certainly do not need to memorize the 140-odd controls, but... Just remember, ISO 27001, one of the most commonly used security control frameworks in the world, defines the best practice recommendations. And if you look in the control document and ask where are the controls, they're listed in an annex A.
[15:28] One final little thing I'm going to cover here. This is not on the exam, but I think this is just useful information to think about. If you look at all these different security control frameworks, and of course, I don't have all of them listed here. If I were to list out more, you'd see even more overlapping. The point I'm trying to show you with this little diagram here is that all of these different security control frameworks, they're not mutually exclusive.
[15:46] It's not like ISO 27001 just covers this stuff, and then COBIT just covers completely different controls over here. That's not at all the case. These different security control frameworks have lots of overlap between them. And the reason I mention this is that a lot of especially larger organizations, they often have to be in compliance with multiple different frameworks.
[16:07] And those frameworks are going to overlap. And this can create quite the compliance burden. If an organization were to sort of be audited externally, or even do their internal audits against all of these, like if they were basically to audit every single framework separately, like go through all the controls that they needed to be in compliance with ISO, and then all the controls they needed to be in compliance with COBIT, and all the controls they needed to be in ITIL, they'd end up retesting the same stuff over and over again. Like access management is a perfect example. All of these control frameworks cover access management. And so if you have to be in compliance with like four or five different frameworks, they're all going to look at access management controls. And so to significantly reduce the audit burden, a good practice is what's often referred to as control rationalization. This is just a fancy way of saying, look at the different control frameworks you have to be in compliance with and identify the overlapping controls.
[17:00] And the whole sort of Holy grail that you want to aim for here is this whole idea of test once report many. So in other words, test your access management controls just a single time. Go through, test all these different controls once, and then use the results of that testing for your ISO compliance, for your PCI compliance, for your HIPAA compliance, whatever it might be. That's the ultimate goal. I'm not going to suggest it's easy to get there. A lot of people do this as a full-time job. I used to do a lot of this back when I worked at Deloitte. We did a lot of consulting around this. It's not easy to do this, but it is worth it. figuring out taking the time to do this because it can significantly reduce a company's compliance burden, how much time they're getting spending getting tested and audited every year. So just want to point that final little thing out there. Okay, there we go. That's security control frameworks.

## Video 10 — Overview of RMC, Security Kernel, & TCB
- Duration: 06:42 (schedule; player total 6:41, header fluctuated 06:42/06:41 across loads)
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/7d792fb6-91ca-4d0d-b9c5-c0e84bd1faba
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Summary Diagram and Explanation of RMC 00:33; Security Kernel 02:53; Trusted Computing Base (TCB) 05:57
- Transcript: 00:13–06:14; 15 blocks / 102 phrase lines (stable across three loads)

[00:13] We're gonna talk about some topics you may have heard about if you've ever been studying for the CISSP. We're gonna talk about the reference monitor concept, the security kernel, and the TCB. And if you've ever read about these, you're probably confused. So let me try and make this as simple as possible for you. It's actually a very easy to understand concept. so let's start with some definitions let's start with a subject what is a subject well we usually refer to a subject as being active so it's a person or a process that's actively trying to access an object so what's an object While an object is whatever is being passively accessed.
[00:54] It could be another process, could be storage, could be SharePoint site, could be a hard drive, could be a database. The point is an object is passive, it's being accessed. And so subjects want access to objects. But if we want to have security, we can never just let any subject access any object and do whatever they want with it because we'd never have security.
[01:18] we have to put something in between the subjects and the objects that will mediate, control the subject's access to an object. And let's just refer to this essentially as a black box or purple box in this case. It can be all sorts of different things as we'll understand in a few minutes here, okay? But the important part is we're putting something in between here that can mediate the access.
[01:41] Now, how does mediation decide what subjects are allowed to access what objects? Well, we have to tell this mediation what to do. we have to program some rules into it. Now, as we've talked about with controls, controls should always have two aspects, a functional aspect and an assurance aspect.
[02:02] So far, we have the functional aspect. We're controlling a subject's access to an object. But we also want to have assurance that this mechanism is working correctly, that the mediation is working correctly. How do we get that assurance? Well, we could log and monitor what's happening here.
[02:19] So this concept of a subject accessing an object through some mediation that is based on a set of rules and all this is logged and monitored is simply the reference monitor concept, the RMC. So if you've ever heard of the RMC, it's as simple as that. A subject accessing an object through a set of rules that are logged and monitored. Now in security, a concept is great, but if we wanna actually have a control that's gonna do something, we can't just have a concept, we need to implement that concept.
[02:53] Whenever you implement the RMC, it's known as a security kernel. So the implementation of the RMC is a security kernel. And where do we have security kernels? Everywhere in security.
[03:08] When you access a building through a lock or a keypad, that's a security kernel. When you type in a username and password, that's a security kernel. When you when an operating system is controlling what applications can access their data in ram or access the network card those are security kernels so we use security kernels everywhere in security Now there's three properties that a good security kernel needs to demonstrate. The first one is known as completeness. And what completeness refers to is that it's impossible to bypass the mediation.
[03:42] So if a subject was somehow able to bypass the mediation, it would completely negate the whole purpose of this. So completeness, a good security kernel, should demonstrate completeness. It should be impossible to bypass the mediation. Every subject should have to go through the mediation to access the object.
[04:01] The next property is what's known as isolation, and it relates to the rules. The rules need to be tamper proof. In other words, only an authorized individual should be allowed to modify these rules. If any subject was just allowed to modify the rules to their heart's content, there'd be no point in having all this because you'd be like, ah, I can't access the file and I'll just change the rules. So tamper, so isolation means the rules are tamper proof. They can only be modified by an authorized individual. The final property is what's known as verifiability.
[04:34] And verifiability is simply, how do we make sure that this whole process is working properly? Well, we achieve it by logging and monitoring what's going on here. And so verifiability, how do we verify our security kernel is working correctly? It's the logging and monitoring. So that's the RMC and the security kernel.
[04:54] Let's talk about one other concept, the TCB. What is the TCB, the Trusted Computing Base? The TCB is the totality of security mechanisms within an architecture. What does that mean? Well, what's an architecture? John has talked about that an architecture is all of the components that make up a system. So if you take any architecture, any system, like let's say a laptop, If you were to look at the architecture of a laptop, there's going to be all sorts of different components in there. There's going to be your hard drive, your CPU, your RAM, your operating system, your applications, your TPM modules.
[05:29] All of these are components within the architecture. a subset of those components are responsible for security. So some of the components are going to be security kernels. Some of them will be anti-malware systems, host-based firewalls, Any of these security controls, any of the components within the architecture that are responsible for security are components within the TCB.
[05:53] So the TCB is the subset of components within an entire architecture that are responsible for security. And so we can draw a line around our reference monitor concept here in our security kernel and say that these are part of our TCB. They're part of our trusted computing base. But are there other components within the TCB? Absolutely. A TCB is made up of much more than just security kernels.
[06:14] It's all of the components within an architecture that are responsible for security. So there you go. That's your quick overview of the RMC security kernel and TCB. And in subsequent modules, we'll get into each of these components in a little more detail.

[00:12] So the two examples I'm going to talk about here are single points of failure and bypass controls. What is a single point of failure? Well, pretty much exactly what it sounds like. It's a point in the system, if it fails,
[00:27] the system fails. So traditionally, let's consider, for example, your computer network. All network traffic in an organization, when it comes into the organization, it goes through a firewall, and then it comes into an internal router.
[00:47] Well, if that firewall goes down, you're pretty much finished. You're not going to get any network traffic in. And if your router goes down, you're not going to get anything anywhere inside your network, you're finished. So that's a classic, a firewall and a router as examples of single points of failure. Their failure leads to failure of the whole system. Now, obviously, how can we reduce the risk from single points of failure? Well, in general, you use redundancy, or diversity, or both. So instead of just having one router, you have two routers, right?
[01:17] one router breaks, the other router takes over. You could have two firewalls, one firewall if it breaks, the other firewall takes over. Alternatively, you could have diversity, so you could, for instance, have one firewall
[01:36] We have two different kinds of firewalls, one router from Cisco, one router from Palo Alto, et cetera. So the idea is if one goes down, another one goes down because of a physical failure or for some reason, routed around. of single points of failure, we in general use redundancy or diversity. Next, let's talk about a bypass control. What's it sound like? Well, it's a point in the make sense. It's a point in the system, quite a the network will flow around a control and completely a million servers here the very system you are—you're not— that I am just trying to describe. in the morning, they can circumvent the— I type in some text, it doesn't go into a very secure site, so they can bypass it.
[02:21] Let's suppose the CEO wants to go and use the company's VPN and they type in some username and password, but actually it's really a hacker who goes and hacks their IP address so that actually the connection between me, the CEO, my laptop, and the company VPN, via hard line and the network, it completely bypasses the firewall.
[02:35] So in general, we can reduce the risk of bypass controls, so you can build in those through the system. So use encryption, for example. So the transmission is so—can be spied upon or you can enforce the use of secure endpoints.
[02:49] So you build in those through the system to protect against bypass controls. Next, let's talk about race conditions, also known as TOCTOU. A race condition occurs when you have a set of initial conditions so you change a serial and then at some point, between the validation of the conditions, and going down as a separate set of conditions, the TOCTOU. That's why it's also called toctou because it's a time of check, time of use vulnerability. So between our time of check and the time of use, something changes. And this is a classic TOCTOU vulnerability.
[03:14] And of course, race conditions, also known as TOCTOU, are vulnerable for example. So, in the time between... check... race conditions can be a lot of them. Now, how do we reduce the risk of race conditions? Well, one classic way to do it is by using programmatic locks. We put locks so that while you're in a transactional mode, while you're updating a file, no one can get to it, no one can update it, no one can change it until that transaction has completed and then we remove the locks.
[03:34] And also, another way to reduce the risk of a race condition is to ensure that the file can only be read by the time of check, okay, and time of use. Another way is very straightforward, in other words, to ensure access to the file can only be done by the person doing something that is going to change the thing. So that is a race condition. And emanations, emanations, emanations. What are emanations?
[03:57] Well, emanations are signals that leak out from devices that communicate information, and they're unintended. So, if, say, I'm sitting and I'm typing a particular information into my computer, whatever that is, my computer is going to emit emissions that relate to what I'm typing onto it. So, for instance, when I type a character, what does the computer do? Well, the keyboard emits some sort of electromagnetic emissions, for instance. And those emissions vary depending on the character you type. Every character you produce is associated with a subtly different pattern of
[04:31] electromagnetic emissions. So, if you can detect those, you can actually tell oh, he's typing this specific letter. And more generally, if you can pick up the electromagnetic emissions of a device, you can quite a bit about what it's doing, what messages it's receiving, and so forth. So, emanations can be a security risk.
[04:52] So we've covered the three main vulnerabilities here in systems, architectures, and designs. So let's talk a little bit about the risks with emanations. Obviously, if you can place a receiver near a device, and pick up on the emissions being generated by the device.
[05:06] You can actually get quite a bit of information from the device into which images, or files, etc. Further, many organizations realize the risks of emanations, and they look to minimize them. They look to install devices inside a Faraday cage.
[05:16] So a Faraday cage is a device which blocks electromagnetic radiation. ## Video 21 — More detail on Hardening
- Duration: 06:12
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/7d5994da-e314-47e0-9877-4b2c480cbf97
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Hardening definition 00:30; Hardening process 01:25; Where do we get the Hardening Guide / Checklist? 02:05; System Administrator & Compliance Function 03:30; Hardening in Production 04:13; Reasons Why We Need to Monitor the Hardening Guide 04:41; Configuration Management 05:38

[00:18] Okay, I just talked about hardening at a high level. I want to go into just a bit more detail about what hardening is all about and why it's so useful and how we use it and et cetera and so forth.
[00:29] So as I mentioned, fundamentally, hardening is all about locking down a system. Because when you go and buy some new application, some new server, operating system, whatever, firewall, let's pick on a firewall. When you go and buy a new firewall, does it come from the manufacturer all locked down, hardened, and super secure? No. Why not? Well, because when you get some brand new application or hardware, you need to be able to log into it for the first time. So there has to be a default username and password. And on the firewall, a bunch of the ports have to be open so you can remotely log into it.
[01:00] And it has to allow any IP address to log into it. When you buy something from a manufacturer, in almost all cases, it's going to come from the manufacturer in a very insecure state. And this is, of course, where hardening comes in. Before you put anything into production, you need to go through a process to lock that thing down, harden it before you put it into production. So that's what I want to talk about in just a little bit more detail here. Hardening is a very simple process. Hardening is just where you essentially go through a checklist of things that you need to do to lock something down to harden it.
[01:34] This list is obviously going to vary depending on what it is, whether it's a Cisco firewall, or a Palo Alto firewall, or the Windows operating system, or the Unix operating system, or whatever. The list is going to vary, but it's going to be stuff like, you know, change the default username and password, install the most recent patches, close these ports, disable that, etc.
[01:57] So fundamentally, hardening is just following a checklist and doing all the things on the checklist to lock something down. where do you get this checklist well one possible source is from the manufacturer themselves so windows microsoft for instance develops hardening and provides hardening guides for their windows operating systems cisco provides hardening guides for a bunch of their different network devices so sometimes you can get a hardening guide from the manufacturer themselves There's also a big open source repository. The CIS, the Center for Internet Security, maintains a big open source database of hardening guides. So you might be able to get one of these things from a manufacturer. You might be able to get it from an open source repository like CIS. Or you might just have to create one from scratch. No matter where you get a hardening guide from, especially if you get it from CIS or the manufacturer, you're going to have to tailor the hardening guide to your exact environment and exactly how you're using that server, application, whatever.
[02:56] Because obviously the way that you harden a system that's being used, like if you're using a web server in this environment with this data in it versus a web server here with this data in it, they're going to need different rules. They're going to need different controls in place to lock them down for different usages of them. So you're going to have to create a hardening guide for each type of system, each business use case for a system. And then, like I said, hardening is actually a very simple process. When you acquire something new, you just, as part of hardening it, you just go through that checklist. Disable the unnecessary services, enable a host-based firewall, whatever. You typically want two people involved in this process. You want the system administrator, the system administrator or the person that's setting the sync thing up, they'll go through and they'll harden it. And maybe they've even created some scripts to automate this.
[03:43] You know, people, system administrators, we're fallible, so are scripts. There might be a mistake in them. So you typically want a second person involved. So after the system administrator has hardened the system, you then usually want a second person, often from the compliance function, that will double check to make sure, okay, were all the steps followed?
[04:00] Has this system been properly hardened before it goes into production? So that's why I usually want two people involved. The person actually doing the hardening and then often someone from the compliance function just doing a final check to say, did we get everything or did we miss something? You can then put now this hardened, locked down device, you can now put it into production.
[04:18] But you're not done with hardening once something's in production. You need to do ongoing monitoring to make sure the system remains hardened for a couple of major reasons. One, you might update your hardening guide. Over time, you might realize, oh, we need to install a new patch, or we need to change this setting, or we need to add a new service account, or whatever. or disable some service account.
[04:40] So over time, your hardening guide will change, will evolve. And so obviously, any new systems you're going to deploy, you want to make sure that they're deployed according to your updated hardening guide. But you've also got to monitor and make sure that any of your systems that are already in production are appropriately updated to maintain compliance with the hardening guide. So that's one reason to do monitoring, to make sure that if you update your hardening guide, you update any of your systems in production. Another reason is that you want to monitor to make sure a system remains in compliance with the hardening guide is that a change might be made to a system in production that takes it out of compliance with the hardening guide. And you want to detect that. Was it one of your own system administrators that did this? Or was it maybe an attacker installing some sort of backdoor that opened up a port on the system? So by doing this ongoing monitoring to make sure a system remains in compliance, it might help you detect if something untoward has happened with your system. So that's why you need to do ongoing monitoring. to make sure that systems that are in production are appropriately hardened.
[05:38] And by the way, all of this fits within overall configuration management, because of course, what is configuration management? Making sure the systems are properly configured before they go into production and then remain appropriately configured while they're in production. So hardening very much fits in within overall configuration management.

For example, there was a series of experiments conducted with lexus nexus in 2000, and in that experiment, they were able to confirm the identity of 15% of the US population based solely on a date of birth, gender, and 5 digit postal code, or zip code. Now, this experiment was repeated years later in 2010, and they were able to confirm the identity of 87% of the US population based solely on date of birth, gender, and 5 digit postal code, or zip code. So the bottom line is if you centralize a bunch of this information in a data warehouse, there's a very real risk that someone can identify you based on the information contained in the database. And so that's something that you should be aware of when you think about data warehouses. The combination of small bits of information that when combined could reveal potentially sensitive information is called inference and aggregation. The combination of small pieces of information that when combined create potentially sensitive information is called inference and aggregation. It can be difficult to distinguish between inference and aggregation, although they are different processes, they produce the same result. Basically the combination of small bits of information into new information is called inference and aggregation. It's a combination of small pieces of information that when combined create potentially sensitive information. So in certain situations, the combination of small bits of information is called inference and aggregation. Whereas in other situations, is called aggregation. The combination of small bits of information into new information is called inference and aggregation.
[10:41] If you want to prevent the risk of inference and aggregation, you can do a number of things. You can use a technique called reduction. Reduction is a technique where data is removed from a database to prevent aggregation. It's sometimes called cell suppression. And it works a lot like in polling... We're going to talk about...
[11:03] that would be the number of times poll -- that is in the database. If you can, There's a couple of techniques that can be used to mitigate the risk of inference and aggregation. Remember that inference and aggregation is the combination of small bits of information into new information, and it can be dangerous.
[11:23] One way to prevent inference and aggregation in a data warehouse is to reduce the risk of inference and aggregation by... one way to prevent the risk... It is important that if you are going to use a database to store information, you make sure that you are doing so in a way that prevents the risk of inference and aggregation. And then there may be other steps to reduce the risk, but the most important thing is to prevent the risk of inference and aggregation by...
[11:54] using a technique called reduction, or cell suppression, or you can use a technique called, query control. So if you want to prevent the risk of inference and aggregation, you can use a certain technique called query control. Query control is a technique where you control the queries that are allowed to be run against the database. So that's how you prevent the risk of inference and aggregation.
