## Video 24 — Industrial Control Systems & Internet of Things (IoT)
- Duration: 07:13 (schedule; player heading 07:12)
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/0632dba9-eb5b-4db5-9400-26e5f4eaa29c
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Industrial Control Systems (ICS) 00:25; Types of ICS 01:08; Reduce risk in Industrial Control Systems 01:52; Internet of Things (IoT) 03:40; Reduce risk of Internet of Things (IoT) 06:00
- Transcript: 00:13–06:53; 15 blocks / 128 phrase lines (stable across three loads)

[00:13] In this module, we're going to talk about industrial control systems and Internet of Things. Let's start with industrial control systems and a definition. What are industrial control systems? Well, industrial control systems are the hardware and the software that control big expensive hardware.
[00:31] So think manufacturing facilities with all those robots that are building cars or iPhones. Or think of water treatment plants or sewage treatment plants or nuclear... reactors, all of these major pieces of expensive hardware are run by what are called industrial control systems. So given that these industrial control systems control very expensive and often very delicate and potentially catastrophic systems if they were to fail, like nuclear power plants or water treatment plants. These systems have to be very secure and very reliable.
[01:08] There's three major types of industrial control systems that you need to know about for the exam, and you do not have to be an expert on these, but you should recognize that These systems here, the names of these systems are examples of industrial control systems. The most common are SCADA systems. You've also got DCS and PLC systems. And actually, remember for the exam, you do not have to memorize all these acronyms.
[01:31] There'll be a little window that you can button that you can click that will show you a pop-up window that contains all sorts of acronyms and what they actually stand for. So you don't have to memorize the exact wording of these things and you won't get definitional type questions on that. So be aware SCADA, DCS, and PLC. These are very common types of industrial control systems. Now, the big risk that we have with industrial control systems is that these are very expensive, very important systems that are controlling often extremely expensive, very delicate systems. And the big security risk is that the hardware, the software that these data systems are running around or these industrial control systems are running on is often very outdated.
[02:13] It's not uncommon to come across industrial control systems that are still running on like Windows NT. It's crazy sometimes. So, Let's start with a question. Why are some of these such critical systems running on such old, antiquated hardware and software?
[02:29] And the answer is these are very, very critical systems that are often highly customized. So it's often very difficult to upgrade the underlying operating system and hardware. And a lot of these big facilities very much run by the philosophy of if it ain't broken, don't fix it because you do not want to cause an outage or cause these systems to malfunction. So in many cases, we have very outdated, very insecure hardware and underlying operating systems that these industrial control systems are running on top of. And yet these are extremely critical systems. So, how do we protect them?
[03:06] How do we reduce the risk related to these systems? The number one best way, and this is what you should remember for the exam, air gap. So, air gapping is the concept that the industrial control system, the network that it's on, should absolutely not be connected to the rest of the corporate network and especially the internet. There should be a physical air gap, literally no connection between the industrial control system network and the rest of the corporate network and especially the internet.
[03:34] So remember air gapping is one of the best ways of reducing the risk of industrial control systems. Now, let's talk about the Internet of Things. What is the Internet of Things? Well, it's all sorts of devices that have been given the ability to connect to the Internet that probably shouldn't be connected to the Internet, like your fridge and your stove and your dishwasher and your light bulb.
[03:57] So here's what's really led to this. The ability to make a little computer chip, basically a little mini computer that has wifi capabilities and can connect to the internet has become unbelievably cheap. We can make these things for a couple of bucks. and as a result it's become extraordinarily cheap to put these capabilities into all sorts of devices and that's exactly what's happening and there's a few big risks here one most of these Internet of Things chips are made by big OEM, original equipment manufacturers. There's just a few manufacturers that make the vast majority of these Internet of Things little computers that enable this capability. And Security is not typically their top priority.
[04:45] It's to make these things to the lowest possible dollar. And so a lot of the Internet of Things devices out there are... wildly insecure, they have big vulnerabilities in them, and a lot of them are actually very difficult to patch. And you combine that with the fact that when you install these capabilities in a lot of devices like a fridge or a stove, I mean, who thinks to patch their fridge? It's not something that people think about. You plug this device in, you connect it to the internet, and then you forget that it's there. You forget it's connected to the internet. And that leads to the next big risk, and that is that these Internet of Things devices can provide a really easy attack platform for a hacker.
[05:27] So I'll give you an example of this. The largest ever denial of service attack in history originated from
[05:38] security cameras. So someone discovered a vulnerability in security cameras, installed some malware that allowed them to remotely control these security cameras, created a giant botnet essentially of these security cameras, and then perpetrated the largest denial of service attack in history from security cameras. That's pretty ironic. And that highlights the risk with these systems.
[06:04] We don't realize they're connected to the internet and we don't think to patch them. And because we've put them often deep inside of our corporate networks or inside of our personal networks, We don't think of the fact that someone may be able to remotely connect to this device, compromise the device, and now they're inside of our environment.
[06:22] So how do we reduce the risk of Internet of Things devices? How do we deal with this problem? Well, it's best to think of Internet of Things devices as a computer system, because that's literally what they are. So all the same principles that you'd apply to protecting any computer system apply to In-Out-Of-Things devices. You need to make sure they're patched. You need to make sure they're configured properly, that they have proper authentication in place. And because it's often difficult to do all of that with a lot of these devices, network segmentation is really important. Put these things on their own network segment.
[06:53] If they truly do need to connect to the internet, put them off in some network where that's all that they can connect to. Or even better yet, don't connect them to the internet. So that's Internet of Things.

[00:19] Super quick little clarification on a bit of terminology here. We just talked about industrial control systems, ICSs. You might also come across the term OT on the exam, operational technology. What's the difference? Operational technology is a broader term that refers to any hardware software technology used to monitor and control physical processes, devices, including industrial control systems.
[00:43] So operational technology is a broader term contained within operational technology is industrial control systems. So ICS is a subset, is part of OT. And industrial control systems obviously specifically focus on the systems that are used to control industrial processes, water treatment plants, robots in a manufacturing facility. pumps and valves in a nuclear power plant.
[01:07] So just remember OT is the broader term and ICS is a subset of that.

[00:14] We've discussed the defining characteristics of cloud. Now let's talk about the different cloud service models, deployment models, and some of the roles related to cloud computing. Now let's start with the service models. You've probably heard of these before. You've heard of infrastructure as a service, platform as a service, software as a service. But let's provide nice, simple, concise definitions of each one and what they provide to you.
[00:36] Because fundamentally, the cloud service models are what services are being offered in the cloud and what services can we consume as a consumer. so let's start with perhaps the easiest to understand software as a service software as a service is this concept that we can rent access to an application that is provided in the cloud typically it's a web-based application And this rental part is important because this is a big shift in how we use software. It used to be software was on premise and we would pay for licenses or we would buy the software. With software as a service in the cloud, one of the big defining characteristics is that you are simply renting access, usually on a monthly basis, or maybe it's even free, to access this software, this application in the cloud. That's all software as a service is. What about infrastructure as a service?
[01:33] We've talked about how in the cloud, we don't have access to any physical hardware, but we have access to all of these virtual resources, compute, network, and storage. So the idea behind infrastructure as a service is you can essentially lift and shift your entire physical data center and move it to the cloud. So all the components that you'd have in a physical data center, firewalls, routers, switches, servers, IDS, IPS appliances, DLP appliances, you can lift and shift all of these physical components to the cloud, but instead of being a physical server or a physical router or a physical switch, it's going to be a virtual router or a virtual switch or a virtual server. And so the idea of infrastructure as a service is you can recreate your entire infrastructure as virtual components in the cloud. So that's the concept of infrastructure as a service. Now the final one is sometimes the most confusing for people. Platform as a service. Let's say that there's, you wanna have access to some software in the cloud and you've looked at all the different software as a service vendors and no one just quite provides the software that you need.
[02:51] So you decide you're gonna have to create your own application. That's what Platform as a Service is for. Platform as a Service provides essentially two major capabilities. It provides a development environment where you can code, create your own application, and also a runtime environment where you can actually run that application.
[03:14] So platform as a service allows users, individuals, organizations to create their own custom applications and then run those applications in the cloud. And oftentimes when you're using software as a service, for instance, what you're actually paying for is some company out there has seen a niche, has seen an opening in the marketplace. They've created through platform as a service, their own custom application, and then they start renting access to that application to other people. And so from those consumers perspective, that actually looks like software as a service. So a lot of the software as a service that's out there, the way it's built is on top of platform as a service.
[03:56] Now, you can find all sorts of edge cases and different types of services that blur the lines between these things, but these are the basic definitions. And there's actually one more that I'll add because it's becoming so incredibly popular nowadays. And that's the idea of containerization or containers as a service. The idea where you place your code, your application within one of these standardized modular containers like a Docker, and then you can run that container in a containerization environment.
[04:25] And so a lot of the big cloud providers nowadays have this concept of containers as a service.

[04:49] And what you'll see here is in infrastructure as a service, there's a lot more pink boxes. And in software as a service, there's almost no pink boxes. They're all purple. They can create their own virtual servers. They can install their own operating systems, configure them exactly as they want them, install their own applications. And the answer is usually no one, because if it's a shared responsibility and it's not clearly documented who's doing what, then there's lots of finger pointing and no one ends up doing it. And so this is something I want to highlight related to cloud security that's so extremely important because most cloud is outsourcing to a service provider.

The service provider is responsible for creating the security kernel, for creating a secure network and operating system and all the components underneath that. Who's responsible for that? Obviously the consumer, only they can know that. So this is a perfect example of a shared responsibility.

[08:04] So deployment models is essentially about where is that hardware. In the public cloud, it means the hardware is at a service provider and it's entirely their equipment and they have control over it. And who has access to the public cloud? Everyone.
[08:21] So public cloud is the most commonly used type of cloud, but it's also the least trusted because it's entirely run by a service provider and it's accessible by anyone. And there are absolutely lots of attackers, malicious individuals, threat agents on the big cloud service providers. They love their systems. So that's public cloud. The other end of the spectrum is private cloud.
[08:46] Private cloud implies that it's only accessible by one consumer. So public cloud means it's accessible by anyone. Private cloud is accessible by only, it's dedicated or accessible to only one consumer. Where is the hardware actually stored? This is where it gets very confusing.
[09:05] Private cloud could be in an organization's own data center with their own systems, entirely private, could be provided by a service provider, can even be things like VPCs, Don't get into all that. Just think of the basic levels. Public cloud, accessible by everyone, less trusted. Private cloud, dedicated to one customer, therefore more trustworthy.
[09:24] You've also got community cloud. A community cloud is where a small number of organizations that have similar needs get together and build a small cloud that they all share. a perfect example of this and in fact the largest example i'm aware of of a community cloud in the world is the us gov cloud the us government has this requirement that any of their agencies that are using the cloud that cloud has to be fed ramp compliant Well, the U.S. federal government went to Amazon and said, hey, we'd like you to make your AWS environment FedRAM compliant.
[09:57] And Amazon said, no thanks, but we'll create an entire dedicated community cloud just for the U.S. government that will be FedRAM compliant. And that's exactly what they did. And so GovCloud is a community cloud accessible only by US government agencies.
[10:13] And then the final thing here, deployment model is what's known as hybrid. And hybrid is simply any combination of the other three. Commonly, it's a combination of private and public. And that's where I'll introduce one final term, and that is cloud bursting.
[10:31] Cloud bursting is the idea that if you run out of resources in, say, your private cloud, you can burst into the public cloud and run some of your systems and store data, say, in the public cloud. It doesn't have to be private to public. It's simply the idea of you run out of resources in one cloud, so you burst into another cloud. But that's the idea of cloud bursting.
[10:52] Now let's talk about some of the roles related to cloud computing. It's extremely important to understand who's responsible and accountable for what. And there's a few roles that you'll have to know about. And frankly, these are very easy to understand. You first of all got your cloud consumer.
[11:08] This is the person or organization that is consuming, using the cloud services. You have your cloud processor. This is the service provider that's providing the services. Could be a third party, could even be another business unit within the same company that's providing the cloud services. there's then all sorts of cloud service partners. And the only really major partner that you need to know about are brokers.
[11:37] Brokers act as intermediaries between a consumer and a cloud service provider. And one of the major things that you need to know about cloud brokers is that they don't offer new or unique services on their own, but they can layer services on top of an existing cloud service provider. I'll give you a perfect example. You've probably heard of this concept, a cloud access service broker, CASBs.
[12:02] A cloud broker can sit between a consumer and the cloud provider. And an example of that would be, let's say that a company has decided that all their employees can use Box. Now, the company may not trust sending their data to Box and having Box encrypt the data, because if that was the setup, if Box was compromised, the attacker would have both the encrypted data and the encryption keys, because if Box was doing the encryption, both of those pieces of data would be there. So the company might decide, you know what, we do want to use Box. We want to have that capability for our employees, but we don't want Box performing the encryption. And we also want to have a little bit of control over what data is being sent to Box. implement some DLP data loss prevention type capabilities.
[12:45] So what an organization might do is use a cloud service broker that would sit between the consumer and box. And one of the key things that the broker could do is they could layer on a service of encryption. So when a consumer goes to send data to Box, it would be intercepted by the broker, the data would be encrypted at the broker, and then just the ciphertext would be sent to Box. And now if Box is compromised, all the attacker has is the ciphertext and not the keys. So that's an example where you might use brokers. And one final piece that we have absolutely already talked about but is worth reiterating here. It's this difference between accountability and responsibilities.
[13:25] When organizations outsource data, processes, systems to the cloud, Who maintains the accountability? Can an organization outsource the accountability they have for protecting data, for instance? And the answer is absolutely not. You cannot outsource accountability.
[13:42] You can only outsource responsibility. And so this is why it's so incredibly important that organizations are very clear on what their accountabilities are and very clearly defining what their cloud provider needs to be responsible for to protect the data of the systems appropriately, because the accountability remains with the owner.

[00:15] Hello everyone, and welcome to this video. Now, let's talk about cloud computing roles and who is who in the cloud ecosystem. So important in the world of cloud computing are three main roles. the cloud service, customer, the cloud service provider, and the cloud service partner. And you really need to know what each is and what are the responsibilities of each. And you're very likely to test, be tested on these for both the CISSP and CCSP exam. So let's break it down one by one. So let's start with the Cloud Service Customer, or CSC, that's the first role. And there's three players again. So the cloud service customer.
[00:45] So this is a person, an organization, or a group seeking to use the cloud services. So it's you as a user. Maybe you as a user of Gmail, right? Or YouTube or Instagram. Those are companies. Those are consumers. They're consumers. All right, moving to the second role.
[01:10] The cloud service provider, or the CSP. And this is basically the provider of the service itself. That is a service you use, right? Providing the actual cloud service to the CSC. That could be websites like YouTube, AWS, Google, or Amazon, social media, big companies, right? So cloud service providers provide the cloud service to the cloud service customers. They are the ones that actually have the infrastructure, in other words. For example, Amazon, Google, Microsoft, Azure, AliCloud, XYZ, Alibaba, etc.
[01:35] CSPs are responsible for actually delivering cloud service to the CSC. So they are the ones that provide the infrastructure. Their responsibility is to make sure you can comfortably use the service. They are the ones that actually run the infrastructure, in essence.
[01:51] The last role, the cloud service partner is your basic third-party partner. They support CSP in delivering cloud services to the CSC. They can be in a cloud service partnership with CSP. They could be, say, like an outsourcer, could help them out.
[02:06] So when you have a third party who is supporting CSP, that is the cloud service partner. For example, McDonald's and Burger King are taking IT from other CSPs. And their provider is not directly providing them with the service. But they have a third party, like a third-party vendor or an IT outsourcing kind of thing, who are probably doing it for them. So that is a cloud service partner. That is a cloud service partner. And I will be able to run all of these organizations, running it efficiently And I will be able to run all of these organizations, running it efficiently provided by the CSP to the CSC. Got it? So that is basically, in a nutshell, the role of the cloud service partner.
[02:42] So really important, really important that you know this, and you understand who the cloud service customer and customer, the CSP's consumer, and the CSP partner are.

One is that the transposition cipher does not replace any characters, Or any bits. What it does is it changes the order of the characters in the plaintext data stream.

And the word asynchronous means that they do not happen at the same time. So for example, a file that is encrypted at a point in time and then decrypted later is asynchronous.

[00:14] One of the most important things that we need to know about with substitution ciphers is that it is critical that we do not use a system that creates patterns that an attacker can use to work out what the plaintext is or indeed what the ciphertext is. So let's look at how we would hide its patterns. Such that an attacker cannot easily work out what plaintext was used. So one of the things that we need to know is that we need to be able to hide patterns. But how?
[00:57] So you take the message gubbins for example. And if we just use a simple substitution cipher to change it into an encrypted form So in this particular case if you look at the whole segment here it's always going to make the same
[01:14] The letter g in the word gubbins is always going to become the letter o in the ciphertext. The letter u is always going to become the letter r. And so on, the letter b is always going to become the letter x. And so on. The letters i, n and s always become y, p and v respectively.
[01:41] And that would create a pattern. Because when we look at the two b's in the word gubbins, the second letter I mean the third and fourth letters they are both b's and they're going to be the same in the ciphertext. Which is obviously a problem because what will happen is that the attacker will be able to spot that they're the same in the ciphertext and that's the pattern they would use to work out what the plaintext might be. And so
[02:16] the answer is to avoid creating patterns. So we hide. the patterns so that the attacker doesn't know what the patterns are. And that's what we're going to do by using something called polyalphabetic ciphers and running ciphers. And also one time pads. And they all hide the patterns. So let's look at that.
[02:43] So we already looked at monoalphabetic ciphers and we said that they create patterns and therefore they're not good. So we hide. the patterns by using a new technique called polyalphabetic ciphers. In the case of polyalphabetic ciphers, what we do is that we have more than one alphabet, not just a single substitution alphabet that we use constantly. The way we do this is that we have four lines.
[03:13] And so in this particular example, the first line is a simply monoalphabetic substitution alphabet. The second line is a second monoalphabetic alphabet. The third is the third substitution alphabet. And the fourth is a fourth substitution alphabet. So we've got four substitution alphabets here. ## Video 42 — Stream vs. Block Ciphers
- Duration: 17:07
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/eb3611d5-a352-42eb-a0b8-c8722602c499
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Types of Ciphers 00:53; Stream Ciphers 02:54; Block ciphers 06:02; Symmetric Block Modes 07:11; Electronic Codebook (ECB) 08:09; Cipher Block Chaining (CBC) 11:48; Cipher Feed Back (CFB) 13:44; Output Feed Back (OFB) 14:15; Counter (CTR) 14:39
- Transcript: 00:12–16:48; 32 blocks / 244 phrase lines (stable across three loads; panel-internal duplicate line at 06:30/06:34 preserved)

[00:12] Right, we've talked about how all symmetric and asymmetric algorithms in cryptography work with bits, not letters like we were showing before. So how they do substitution and transposition and ultimately turn things into ciphertext is by using bits. Now if you think about that, once we turn something into bits and it needs to be encrypted, we have two options on how we can work with those bits. We can work with each bit one at a time as a stream or we can work with chunks of bits at the same time and that's what we call block ciphers.
[00:53] So the two types of ciphers that exist out there that can take any data and turn it into ciphertext are either stream ciphers or block ciphers and that's what we're going to talk about in this section and what's important for you to remember are things like which ones would be fastest, where would it make sense to use block as opposed to stream and so on. So let's look at these. different types of ciphers that exist out there. So first of all, stream ciphers work with one bit at a time as a stream, whereas block will take chunks of bits and then do certain creative things with each of those chunks that are called blocks.
[01:40] So it would make sense that we would use stream ciphers where we process one bit at a time anyway. So technologies like ultimately across a network what travels across the wires that connect devices together is one bit at a time. If we need to encrypt that bit then it would make sense that we would use stream ciphers. Block ciphers are typically software-based encryption algorithms.
[02:10] The ones that you've heard of like DES and Triple DES and the AES and so on. Those are considered to be block ciphers because they drive encryption through software. They are software-based applications. And so the advantages and disadvantages of each would be that stream would be faster because we can process and encrypt one bit at a time.
[02:35] Whereas block ciphers need to have the logic to be able to fill a block and then do something with it and then fill the next block. and do something with it and keep going until we encrypt all of the information. So that's the idea behind stream ciphers and block ciphers. Now there are stream cipher algorithms that exist out there and basically the only one that is significant that exists out there is called RC4.

## Video 45 — Diffie-Hellmann Key Exchange Protocol
- Duration: 09:52
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/086affa2-3846-4262-bff3-61218f126177
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Diffie-Hellmann Key Exchange Protocol 02:59
- Transcript: 00:13–09:22; 17 blocks / 141 phrase lines (stable across three loads; title spells "Hellmann" while the transcript text spells "Diffie-Hellman" — both preserved as displayed)

[00:13] We've talked about the advantages and disadvantages of symmetric and asymmetric key cryptography and one of the things that we've learned and we know is that symmetric key cryptography is the only thing that we can use for anything that requires speed and bulk. And so if we use an example such as VPNs, VPNs can only use symmetric key cryptography. When you think how much data and how fast it needs to be transmitted across those VPN solutions that all of us use, to use remote access to connect back into our internal networks. Your VPN client which is on your laptop and the VPN server that exists at the other end in your company's network really can only use symmetric key cryptography.
[01:04] That means you have to have the same key at both ends for that particular session. and if you disconnect that vpn session for whatever reason and reconnect again two minutes later let's say guess what the same key has to be at both ends but it has to be different than the keys that were used in the previous session. And if there are other people traveling around that work for the same company and are connected back into the same VPN server at the company, guess what? Their key needs to be the same at both ends but it needs to be different.
[01:40] than the one that you use. So this is a really fundamental problem that all VPN solutions have to solve. And so how do we solve this? How do we get that same key at both ends without having anybody else know in between what that key is? That's a really hard problem to solve and the answer to this is really mathematical magic but when you think about all VPN solutions out there they use this method to generate a the same secret at both ends that can be used as the same symmetric key that can encrypt and decrypt information for that session.
[02:20] And in fact, this is why we call it session keys. A session key is symmetric keys that are used at both ends but they're only used for that particular session. So let's say you're traveling around and you get to Bora Bora and you need to get back into your company's network. You activate your VPN client on your machine and somehow the same key has to be at your end and at the VPN server.
[02:50] Now obviously we can't send that key across the network because anybody sitting in between may be able to see what that key is. So the only way that we can make this work is to use mathematical magic and I'm going to show you what this looks like on the screen here. So let's say that we'll use Alice and Bob as examples but the process by which this is actually done, this mathematical magic is really known as the Diffie-Hellman Key Exchange Protocol because Diffie-Hellman Key Exchange Protocol was basically the first ones to come up with this type of activity that allows us to create and come up with the same secret at both ends that can be used as the symmetric keys that are going to be same at both ends. So let's look at the example here. Let's say Alice is in Bora Bora wants to connect back in with Bob who's sitting at the VPN server at the other end.
[03:51] The first step in this mathematical magic is that they both come up with a totally random number. So let's say Alice comes up with a totally random number. In this case it's the number 7. Bob needs to do the same thing at his end.
[04:09] He comes up with a totally random number. Let's say it's the number 3. Now at this point they've each come up with a secret number. Alice doesn't know what Bob's number is and Bob doesn't know what Alice's number is.
[04:24] But it doesn't matter at this point because the next step is very important. But what they each do is they take those secret numbers that they've each come up with and they put them through a mathematical function, an algorithm, to come up with the result. Now let's use something really simple here just to show the concept, but in real VPN solutions and in real key management protocols, this math would be much more complex. But to show you the concept, we'll use something very simple like times two.
[04:57] So what Alice does is she takes the random number that she came up with, and multiplies it by 2 to come up with 14. Now Bob does the same thing at his end. He takes his random number that he came up with, which was 3, multiplies it by 2, the result is 6. Now they each have two numbers that are related to each other mathematically.
[05:23] Now what happens here is very important. What they each do is they exchange these second numbers that they've just come up with. So Bob is gonna send the six to Alice, and Alice is going to send the 14 to Bob and that's what we're going to show here and then what happens is when they each receive those numbers that they just got from each other they relate them back to the original random numbers that they each came up with so in this case Alice is going to take the 6 that she's just received from Bob and multiply it with her original number. And so if you multiply 6 times 7, she's going to get 42.
[06:08] And Bob is going to do the same thing at his end. he's going to take the number that he just received from Alice, which was the 14, and multiply it with his random number that he originally came up with. So 14 times 3 is going to equal 42 at both ends. Through this mathematical, magical process here, they've been able to generate the same secret at both ends that really represents the symmetric session key that's going to be used by both of them to encrypt and decrypt information in both directions.
[06:45] And that represents the symmetric key that will be used for that session. Now, if they disconnect for whatever reason, and reconnect again, guess what's gonna happen? They're gonna go through this process again, but they're gonna come up with totally different random numbers to start this process. But once they go through this entire process again, it will always generate the same secret at both ends.
[07:11] which will represent that symmetric session key. Now this process here is in fact the Diffie-Hellman Key Exchange Protocol. They were the first ones to come up with this mathematical process that really generates the same symmetric key at both ends and that is referred to as a key management protocol. Now these days there are a whole bunch of these that exist out there.
[07:37] They basically go through the same concept to come up with the same secret. The only difference typically is the math. that is used here. Now we use times two as the math.
[07:49] The math needs to be the same at both ends but that's why for example when you're traveling around your VPN client is the specific solution that your company has put on your laptop. The math obviously has to be the same at both ends which matches up with the math that is at the VPN server. Now there's all kinds of versions of these. Diffie-Hellman was the first one but there exists a whole bunch of other ones out there. Now the only other thing that we need to think about here is that if the math is very simple like times two this would not be secure because once you exchange these numbers If there's a man in the middle that intercepts the numbers, because times two is not a one-way math function, the man in the middle would be able to figure out what the original number is and therefore be able to figure out what the ultimate session key is.
[08:49] So to make this very secure, what we need to do is change the math here, have it be the same at both ends, but it needs to be a one-way mathematical process. In other words, you can only go this way, not the other way. So if there's a man in the middle that intercepts the second numbers that they exchanged, it won't matter because they won't be able to go backwards to figure out the original number. and therefore they won't be able to figure out the ultimate number that represents the session key.
[09:22] So if we make this math a one-way mathematical function, this becomes a secure process. and that's what all key management protocols out there really use in order to generate the same secret session key at both ends such as VPN solutions.

[00:13] We've talked about the advantages of symmetric key cryptography. And just to remind you, they are that symmetric algorithms are really fast. In fact, they can encrypt to the tune of hundreds of millions of bits per second. But the problem that symmetric has is key distribution.
[00:32] asymmetric is the opposite isn't it the advantage of asymmetric is that it gets rid of the problem of key distribution but introduces that slowness problem and so that's why today all crypto systems and solutions that exist out there are actually what we call hybrid. And hybrid simply means that they use both symmetric and asymmetric key cryptography algorithms for what they're both good at. And so what you need to remember here is that symmetric is usually used for bulk and speed. Anything that requires a lot of encryption, a lot of decryption and wherever we need to do it really quickly.
[01:16] Asymmetric algorithms are used to exchange these symmetric keys that are required at both ends in order to be able to do that. So today all cryptography solutions implementations are what we call hybrid and then what they'll do is they'll also add functionality to address hashing algorithms for integrity digital signature algorithms to address non-repudiation and that's what we call crypto system really hybrid products that support all kinds of different algorithms to achieve really the five services that cryptography can for us. But let's look at an example here of hybrid cryptography by using again Alice and Bob. So let's say Alice wants to send a very large message to Bob.
[02:06] Because it's a very large message, the only option that they can use is symmetric cryptography. And so let's say they've decided to use DES for this. So what Alice does is she's going to encrypt that very large message with DES, which requires her to choose one of the possible keys, symmetric keys, that Des uses. Now she knows that the only way that Bob will be able to decrypt that message is to have the exact same symmetric key.
[02:41] So that's the problem of key distribution. that we've talked about but Alice knows a good way to do this and send it to Bob securely because what she can do is she can take that 56-bit symmetric key that Bob requires and encrypt it with Bob's public key. All right, because if Alice encrypts that symmetric key with Bob's public key, that ensures that the only person out there that can decrypt that is Bob with his private key that he keeps private to himself and so now when he receives that he will be able to decrypt it with what no one else has which is his private key at which point he will have the same symmetric key that Alice used in order to encrypt that very large message and Bob now will be able to decrypt very quickly as well because ultimately they both used symmetric key cryptography to be able to encrypt and decrypt that message quickly. But they've used asymmetric key cryptography to be able to exchange the symmetric key that is required at both ends.
[03:59] So this is a perfect example of what hybrid cryptography looks like today. Simply means we use a combination of symmetric and asymmetric for what they're each good at. Symmetric always used for bulk and speed, asymmetric always used to exchange symmetric keys that are required at both ends.
[04:24] Thank you.

In fact a collision is what we're trying to show you here on this slide where you have two different messages that produce the exact same digest and that's what we refer to as collision And good hashing algorithms will and should resist that as much as possible, especially these ones and the better versions of SHA that are collected within that SHA-3 algorithm that we talk about there. [14:10] And if you know anything about this, it goes something like this. And as you start adding more people in the room, the opportunity or the probability that two people share the same birthday in that room will actually grow exponentially to the point where you only need 60 people in a room to have the probability be 99% that two, any two of those people in that room share the same birthday. And by birthday we mean month and day, not year. And so this is referred to as the birthday paradox. You can Google it and there's all kinds of videos that have been made about this. Pretty cool math.

[00:15] We've talked about digital certificates, which we use to verify the owner of a public key. And we've talked about the entity that you go to to get a digital certificate is the certificate authority. And the certificate authority will encrypt and create your digital certificate by encrypting some of your information with the root CA's private key. And all that's true, but there's actually a little more detail to it than that. So let's kind of dig into it here and you'll learn about why we talk about something known as the root of trust and the trust anchor.
[00:46] So while it's true that ultimately the key that signs, that encrypts your digital certificate is ultimately the root CA's key, there's a few more entities involved beneath that. And this is actually why we call it the root of trust because there's actually a tree with the root certificate at the top of that tree and then subordinate CAs beneath that. ## Video 53 — Public Key Infrastructure
- Duration: 08:59
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/2219f21f-bdca-49f1-85ca-87b5b0c3fd89
- Chapters: Public Key Infrastructure 00:00; PKI diagram 01:05; Components of PKI 07:40; Certificate Authority (CA) 07:42; Registration Authority (RA) 07:55; Intermediate / Issuing CA 08:00; Certificate DB 08:06; Certificate Store 08:16; Importance of PKI 08:24
- Transcript: 00:08–08:19; 18 blocks / 170 phrase lines (stable across six loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): PKI is presented as the set of components that together let parties trust who they are communicating with online — without it, data could still move, but modern banking/shopping-style internet use would not work. Walking the PKI diagram from the user up: a user needs a certificate plus a public/private key pair; certificates are not obtained directly from a CA in one step — the Registration Authority first verifies the requester's identity, then the CA issues the certificate, which is recorded in the certificate database and placed in the certificate store. The hierarchy runs root CA → intermediate CA → issuing/client CA → end user, forming the chain of trust. Component roles as drilled: CA = issues certificates; RA = verifies identity; intermediate/issuing CA = issues down the chain; certificate DB and certificate store hold issued certificates. Closing point: a PKI is a system used to manage digital certificates, and that management is what makes trusted communication on the internet possible. Panel quirks noted by the puller: a run of repeated "hello" lines in the 05:44 block and fragmented/duplicated phrasing in several blocks, as displayed on the site.

## Video 54 — S/MIME
- Duration: 02:17 schedule (02:18 on lesson page)
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/96a1e358-5615-43f3-9b44-0a9505ceb9b6
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Multipurpose Internet Mail Extensions (MIME) Headers 00:23; S/MIME 01:06
- Transcript: 00:19–01:34; 4 blocks / 32 phrase lines (stable across six loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): MIME headers identify the nature/format of what is being transmitted (text, HTML, JPEG, etc.); the mechanism began with email but is also used in other protocols such as HTTP. S/MIME is the secure extension for transmitting that MIME data, and it relies on both digital certificates and digital signatures — certificates to authenticate the other party and supply the public key, signatures to provide integrity and authenticity of the data sent. Site garbles noted by the puller and preserved here as flags only: "FMIME" and "Secure Multipurpose Internet Mail Exchanges" appear in the panel as displayed.

## Video 55 — Key Management
- Duration: 09:40
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/0d672d4d-1d70-4370-895b-80edc3728e5a
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Key Management 00:13; Kerckhoffs' Principle 00:24; Key Management Activities 00:48; Key Generation 01:06; Key Distribution 02:28; Key Storage 03:52; Key Rotation 05:48; Key Recovery 06:43; Key Disposition / Destruction 08:07
- Transcript: 00:09–09:15; 18 blocks / 167 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): Opens on Kerckhoffs' principle — a cryptosystem stays secure even when everything about it is public except the key, so the whole discipline is about managing keys well. Generation: pick keys randomly from the key space with an automated process; people are poor random sources and computers are only pseudo-random, but pseudo-random generators are good enough. Distribution options: physical courier, key wrapping (a bundle of symmetric keys encrypted under a key-encrypting key so only the KEK needs secure transfer), and hybrid cryptography. Storage: a TPM is a hardware chip on an endpoint that holds keys (e.g. for full-drive encryption, so the key never sits on the drive itself); an HSM does the same job for an entire network. Rotation: change keys periodically or on suspected compromise; frequency depends on the value/criticality of the system, from seconds for some VPNs to yearly for others. Recovery has three methods: split knowledge (the key's knowledge is divided among people), dual control (two people must act together, e.g. key plus PIN), and key escrow (keys held by a trusted third party). Destruction: crypto shredding — encrypt data strongly and destroy every copy of the key — is the practical destruction method for cloud data, where physically destroying shared media is impossible.

---

## Video 56 — Cryptography Summary
- Duration: 17:37
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/5a68ec4b-51f0-4a8c-953f-a9b0f8e67ea3
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Putting it all together 01:26
- Transcript: 00:18–16:58; 43 blocks / 312 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): A recap of the whole cryptography section. Note: this summary video lists the five crypto services as confidentiality, integrity, authentication, non-repudiation and **availability** — video 38 listed access control as the fifth; the guide wording governs. Core distinctions drilled: hashing validates integrity and must never be used to hide data (use encryption for that); symmetric crypto uses one shared key, is fast, and its real challenge is key distribution/management — DES 56-bit key / 64-bit block, 3DES 168-bit key, AES 128-bit block with 128/192/256-bit keys, and the symmetric key-count formula n(n−1)/2; asymmetric crypto uses public/private pairs, needs 2n keys, is slower, and removes the pre-shared-key problem — RSA (security from factoring), ECC (smaller keys for equal security), Diffie-Hellman (key exchange/agreement without transmitting the symmetric key). Ephemeral (per-session) keys give perfect forward secrecy: one compromised session key exposes only that session. Digital signatures use the signer's private key and provide authentication, integrity and non-repudiation. Hashing: message digest = one-way hash value; SHA (NSA-developed, NIST-published) sizes — SHA-1 160, SHA-256 256, SHA-384 384, SHA-512 512 — with SHA-1 being phased out (short digest, demonstrated collisions); HMAC/MACs add a shared secret key to hashing for integrity. Hybrid systems tie it together: asymmetric crypto distributes a symmetric session key, symmetric crypto does the bulk encryption; certificates bind identities to public keys via a CA (root of trust / trust anchor). Closes by recapping the key-management activities from video 55 (generation, distribution, storage incl. TPM vs HSM, rotation, recovery incl. M-of-N, destruction / crypto shredding). Panel garbles flagged by the puller include "symmetric photography", "PubMed" and "non-reputation" — substance reliable, exact wording not.

## Video 57 — Cryptanalysis: Cryptanalytic Attacks
- Duration: 07:47 schedule (player showed 7:46 on all loads; the lesson-header duration label flipped between 07:46 and 07:47 across loads — transcript itself unaffected)
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/e0068e1f-1451-4c5f-8108-ba3923479db2
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; What is Cryptanalysis? 00:13; Types of Cryptanalysis Attacks 00:36; Goal of Cryptanalysis Attacks 00:51; Brute Force Attack 01:39; Ciphertext Only Attack 05:02; Plaintext Attack 05:18; Chosen Plaintext Attack 06:06; Chosen Ciphertext Attack 06:44; Other types of Cryptanalysis Attacks 07:01
- Transcript: 00:14–07:22; 19 blocks / 128 phrase lines (transcript stable across three loads; no alternate version — only the header duration label differed)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): The single goal of every cryptanalytic attack is to deduce the key. Brute force tries every key in the key space; each added key bit doubles the space, so modern key lengths make it absurd — illustrated with the EFF's 1999 Deep Crack machine brute-forcing a DES key in ~20 hours and a 256-bit key space (~10^77) being comparable to counting atoms in the observable universe. Attack types are defined by what the analyst can access: ciphertext-only (hardest — ciphertext only), known-plaintext (both plaintext and its ciphertext; the key is still the prize because it unlocks other messages and enables forgery), chosen-plaintext (attacker feeds chosen plaintext into the encryption process and studies the ciphertext), chosen-ciphertext (attacker feeds chosen ciphertext and studies the resulting plaintext). Linear and differential cryptanalysis are flagged as "complicated math" approaches, and a factoring attack points to exactly one algorithm: RSA.

---

## Video 58 — Cryptographic Attacks, Man-in-the-middle, Replay and Pass the Hash Attacks
- Duration: 04:56
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/f3ff9285-72f7-4068-8829-8c1cacecaf19
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Cryptographic Attacks 00:24; Man-in-the-middle 00:44; Replay attack 01:36; Pass the Hash 04:09; Review 04:27
- Transcript: 00:15–04:32; 9 blocks / 90 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): Cryptographic attacks are a mixed family with no single goal, unlike cryptanalytic attacks (video 57), which all aim at deducing the key. The hierarchy taught here: a man-in-the-middle attack is the generic pattern — an attacker sits between two communicating parties and can read, alter or block the traffic. A replay attack is a type of man-in-the-middle: the attacker records cryptographic material they cannot decipher and re-sends it later to gain access. Pass-the-hash is a type of replay attack: hashing a password is one-way, so a captured hash does not reveal the password — but if the server accepts the hash itself as proof of identity, the attacker can simply present the captured hash and be authenticated as that user without ever knowing the password.

---

## Video 59 — Temporary File, Implementation, Fault Injection and Side Channel Attacks
- Duration: 13:32
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/26fac3ca-2c81-4b38-af6e-e591113dc1ad
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Temporary file attacks 00:21; Implementation attacks 01:36; WEP 02:06; RC4 02:35; Short & Static IV 03:15; TKIP 04:18; Fault injection 05:05; Voltage glitching 05:29; Clock glitching 05:40; Laser injection 06:12; Electro-magnetic injection 06:25; Side channel attacks 06:45; Timing attack 07:26; Power attack 09:00; Radiation emissions 09:29; Example side channel attack 10:13
- Transcript: 00:15–12:57; 32 blocks / 255 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): Four attack families. (1) Temporary-file attacks: keys normally sit in protected hardware (TPM/HSM), but during actual encryption the CPU copies the key and data into cache, registers or RAM — an attacker who reaches that temporary copy steals the key. (2) Implementation attacks: the algorithm is sound but its implementation is flawed. The exam example is WEP: it runs on RC4, which has no major flaw itself, but WEP's IV was partly static and too short, making WEP easily broken; TKIP was the interim fix before WPA/WPA2 (more in Domain 4). (3) Fault-injection attacks aim to disrupt rather than steal: voltage glitching, clock glitching, laser injection and electromagnetic injection all induce faults that corrupt data or crash the system. (4) Side-channel attacks: no login and no direct access — the attacker observes the hardware itself. Timing attacks measure how long computations take, power attacks measure power draw, and radiation-emission attacks pick up electromagnetic emanations; all use precise measurement plus statistical analysis to infer keys (DES's 16 rounds used as the timing example). The instructor stresses these are nation-state/research-lab level. Closing examples Row Hammer and RAM bleed (memory bit-flips leaking across rows — a cloud cross-tenant worry) are explicitly flagged as NOT on the exam.

---

## Video 60 — Dictionary Attack, Rainbow Tables & Salting
- Duration: 17:27
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/2d21549d-6669-4542-a814-951b63e60024
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Dictionary Attack 00:28; Rainbow Tables 03:03; Salting 16:22 (chapter marker sits at the closing slides; salting is actually taught from ~09:08)
- Transcript: 00:19–17:25; 50 blocks / 300 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version; final block is a single truncated word as displayed)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): Dictionary attacks try likely passwords first rather than brute-forcing the whole character set — originally literal dictionaries matched to the target's language (with leet-speak substitutions anticipated), today compiled lists of the most common passwords from mass breaches. Password storage, in ascending order of safety: plaintext is never acceptable; encryption (e.g. AES-256) is better but two-way, so a stolen key exposes everything; hashing is the correct approach because it is one-way. The weakness of plain hashing is the rainbow table: a pre-computed table of common passwords run through common hash functions (MD5, SHA-1) that lets an attacker look up a stolen hash and recover the password almost instantly — the instructor notes his drawing is technically a hash table, but at CISSP's management level it is called a rainbow table. Salting defeats it: append a large random value, unique per user, to the password before hashing, so the resulting hash appears in no pre-computed table. Salts are stored in plaintext and that is fine — a known salt only forces the attacker to build a fresh table for that one user, which takes months to years, pushing them back to per-user brute force. A pepper differs: one shared value applied to all users, so it is weaker than a per-user salt. Closing trivia, explicitly NOT on the exam: bcrypt is a deliberately inefficient, tunable hashing algorithm — cheap for one login, prohibitively expensive for building a rainbow table.

---

## Video 61 — Birthday Attack, Kerberos Exploitation, and Social Engineering Attacks
- Duration: 05:23
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/520c5ffe-c037-40ab-93a6-e409793a5904
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Birthday attacks 00:22; Kerberos exploitation 01:19; Kerberos IV 02:04; Kerberos V 02:33; Social engineering attacks 03:23; Purchase Key attack 03:43; Rubber Hose attack 04:13
- Transcript: 00:16–04:48; 14 blocks / 93 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): Closing crypto-attacks video. Associate four ideas together: integrity ↔ hashing ↔ collisions ↔ birthday attack (an efficient way to find collisions, undermining hash integrity — same concept as video 48). Kerberos (full treatment comes in Domain 5) is an old, pervasive single-sign-on protocol, effectively the default because it is built into so many systems. Version contrast to know: Kerberos v4 (1980s) supports only DES with a 56-bit key — now insecure and quickly brute-forced; v5 (1990s, most recent) adds Triple DES and IDEA. Rule of thumb given: keys longer than 80 bits are reasonably secure. Kerberos's structural weakness is that it is symmetric crypto, so secure key exchange is hard at realm scale. Final two named attacks, whose standard names the instructor says the exam expects: purchase-key attack (bribe someone for the key) and rubber-hose cryptanalysis (coerce/torture someone for crypto material).

---

## Video 62 — Intro to Physical Security
- Duration: 11:14
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/dbed33b6-f678-498f-9c64-75b16ea0f994
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Goals of Physical Security 00:32; Primary goal 01:36; Physical security controls 02:21; Threats 03:45; Natural/environmental 04:02; Utility Systems 04:23; Human-made / Political events 04:33; Example physical security threats 06:50; Dumpster Diving 06:53; Shoulder Surfing 07:29; Physical security systems and methods 08:30; Layered defense model 08:37
- Transcript: 00:15–10:53; 23 blocks / 221 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): Physical security underpins the whole CIA triad (power, network, temperature/humidity drive availability and integrity), and the guiding principle is that physical access effectively means ownership of a system — built-in bypasses make logical access easy once someone can touch the hardware. The primary goal of physical security, overriding everything else, is the safety of people. Physical-control functions are deter/prevent, delay, detect, assess, respond — and each layer still needs preventive + detective + corrective elements (locks only delay; there is no unpickable lock). Threats come in three categories: natural/environmental (force majeure — hurricanes, floods, tornadoes, earthquakes), utility systems (the three core utilities are power, network, HVAC), and human-made/political events (people are the biggest concern). Examples given: a riot fire beside a telecom data centre whose exploding oil-cooled transformers surged and fried protected equipment; dumpster diving in its modern form — buying retired equipment (a police department sold copiers/printers with unwiped drives holding hundreds of thousands of sensitive records); shoulder surfing, now also done via high-resolution PTZ cameras. Defence in depth: stack complete layers (fence / tripwires / guards, then walls / cameras / guards, then doors and zones down to the data-centre floor). Entrances/exits: zero would be most secure but unworkable, and life safety (fire egress) deliberately trades security for multiple exits.

---

## Video 63 — How do we decide which physical security controls to put in place? & Perimeter
- Duration: 06:26
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/6845066f-eddc-4c25-9fa8-2b3644fc27b6
- Chapters: How do we decide which physical security controls to put in place? 00:00; Risk Management Process 01:10; Security Survey 01:28; Target Identification 01:44; Threat Definition 02:01; Facility Characteristics 02:17; Site Planning 02:48; Site Planning Goals 02:58; Higher Value Areas 03:25; Perimeter 03:58; Landscaping 04:06; Grading 04:53; Bollards 05:22
- Transcript: 00:15–06:12; 9 blocks / 131 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): Budgets are not unlimited, so control selection reuses the risk-management process (identify valuable assets and their risks; avoid/transfer/mitigate/accept). In physical security that process is called the security survey, with three steps: target identification (which assets — data centres, hardware, other physical assets), threat definition (which threats apply, given location — flood, hurricane, fire, theft), and facility characteristics (facility vulnerabilities, e.g. wood-frame ↔ fire, floodplain ↔ flooding). Choosing the mitigating controls is site planning; its goals are, first, life safety above all, then protecting high-value assets and key operations — so spend concentrates on higher-value areas (bank vault, police evidence locker, wiring closets, data-centre floor). Perimeter controls introduced: landscaping kept back from the building to preserve sight lines and deny tree-climbing roof access; grading sloped away from the building so water drains off; bollards to stop vehicles being driven through walls/doors (post-Oklahoma-City federal buildings, embassies, the red balls outside Target stores). Later videos will cover cameras, locks, doors, windows, generators, UPS and fire suppression.

## Video 64 — Physical Security Controls
- Duration: 16:20 schedule (16:19 on lesson page)
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/0634d69b-f8ef-4531-97a2-15e9cc101d9a
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Close Circuit TV (CCTV) 00:25 [spelled "Close" on the page]; Passive Infrared Devices 02:44; External Monitoring / Lighting 05:47; Interior Monitoring 07:28; Doors 08:57; Mantraps 12:18
- Transcript: 00:18–15:52; 33 blocks / 330 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): CCTV — detailed transmission-media/camera-quality material is no longer tested; what matters is control type. A camera system can be deterrent, detective and audit, but if forced to pick one primary type the answer is detective. Passive infrared (PIR) sensors detect changes in the infrared signature between rapid snapshots; warm bodies radiate IR, and the sensor must be recalibrated for ambient temperature (in a hot Phoenix summer a person registers as a decrease in IR, not an increase). Lighting serves safety (car parks after dark), deterrence, and detection — it keeps cameras in colour/day mode instead of grainy black-and-white night mode and helps guards see. Badges vs card readers are distinct controls: a badge is a printed photo card checked visually by a guard; a card reader is electronic (unique number / smart-chip challenge, magstripe swipe or proximity). Doors: door and frame are only as strong as the weaker of the two, and external hinges are a classic weak point (hinge pins can be pulled to remove a locked door). Mantraps (exam term, though the instructor dislikes it) prevent tailgating/piggybacking, in two styles: a turnstile cylinder that stops part-way and demands a second factor (fingerprint/iris, sometimes floor weight sensors — often banned in Europe on privacy grounds), and a double-door room where the first door must close before the second opens and a camera/guard verifies a single occupant — the room style also fits equipment carts, so data centres often run both.

---

## Video 65 — Locks
- Duration: 06:10 schedule (06:09 on lesson page)
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/539f8006-1165-46ff-9bcb-3a2be99105c5
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Locks 00:26; Types of Locks 00:53; Mechanical Key Lock 01:07; Mechanical Combination Lock 01:45; Mechanical Magnetic Lock 01:57; Major Downside of Mechanical Locks 02:28; Electronic Lock 02:45; Proximity / RFID 03:08; Electronic Combination 03:47; Bio-metric 04:33; What determines the security of a combination lock? 05:00; Card Access / Biometrics 05:42
- Transcript: 00:15–05:38; 12 blocks / 112 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): No lock is unpickable — locks are delay controls, not prevention. Two exam categories: mechanical and electronic. Mechanical types: keyed (weakness is key management — a lost key or a departing employee's kept key can force a full rekey), combination (dial sequence), and magnetic (e.g. clothing tags released by a strong counter magnet). The shared downside of all mechanical locks is no built-in logging or monitoring — that must be added separately (e.g. a camera). Electronic locks log who opened what and when, by combination, proximity/RFID card, or biometric. But combinations and cards do not prove who actually used them (a found card works for a stranger); biometrics (fingerprint, facial, iris, retinal, hand geometry) uniquely identify the individual — detail deferred to Domain 5. Exam-flagged: what determines the security of a combination lock is the complexity of the combination — not the metal, not the tumbler count.

---

## Video 66 — Windows
- Duration: 05:01 schedule (05:00 on lesson page)
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/b3db4646-3ab9-4a5b-81c0-2ab4961b97e0
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Windows 00:16; Types of Window Material 00:29; How to detect a Broken Window? 03:42; Glass Break Sensor 03:56; Shock Sensor 04:10
- Transcript: 00:03–04:52; 14 blocks / 90 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): Window security turns on material and break detection. Materials, weakest to strongest: plate glass (cheapest, breaks into dangerous large shards); tempered glass (breaks into small pebble-like pieces — cuts, not impalement); wired glass (embedded wires hold broken glass together, so nobody can reach through to a door knob); laminated glass (sandwiched layers, typically glass–plastic–glass, as in car windshields — much stronger); polycarbonate (plastic; the strongest — the exam answer for strongest glass — and what most bullet-resistant glass is made of; there is no truly "bulletproof" glass). Solar films are a coating, not a glass type: they block UV/heat (cutting cooling costs) and can create one-way-mirror effects. Break detection: glass-break sensors listen for the sound of breaking glass (one sensor covers a room's windows); shock sensors attach to each window and detect vibration. Exam-style closer: in a noisy, occupied room the shock sensor wins, because a glass-break sensor may not hear the break over the noise. Panel quirk: the transcript opens with a stray one-word caption at 00:03, preserved in the puller's notes.

---

## Video 67 — Walls
- Duration: 02:41
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/a35074a6-5bd6-4590-9cea-892297a8d19e
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Walls 00:13; Intrusion Detection Systems 02:07
- Transcript: 00:14–02:30; 6 blocks / 43 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): Walls are a delay barrier, never an absolute one — a determined attacker with enough time gets through anything. Lightweight stud-and-drywall partitions delay only seconds. A real barrier wall runs full height to the true ceiling/roof (not just to a drop ceiling, which leaves a climb-over gap) and is anchored top and bottom; partitions can be strengthened with internal mesh or rebar, which slows but does not stop entry. Tempered-glass walls are barriers with limited security value since they can be broken, and the specified construction grade matters because contractors may substitute cheaper materials. The closing section covers physical intrusion detection systems — explicitly not network/logical IDS: motion detectors, contact switches (door/window opening), glass-break detectors and vibration sensors; their job is detection that triggers investigation and response.

## Video 68 — Skimming
- Duration: 02:36
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/dcecab7e-be9e-4630-b41d-192422b1594b
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Skimming 00:22; Example of Skimming 01:11
- Transcript: 00:15–02:18 (last block 01:35); 4 blocks / 47 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version; captions heavily fragmented but no term-changing garble)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): Skimming is a physical attack on card readers: a hidden overlay device (plus a fake keypad) fitted over a legitimate reader — chiefly ATMs and gas pumps — copies magnetic-stripe data while the keypad overlay captures the PIN, giving the attacker everything needed to clone the card and drain the account. Flagged as exam material. The practical half of the lesson is recognition: in the instructor's own ATM example, the reader protruded further and felt thicker than usual and the keypad felt spongy and thick — a reader or keypad that looks, sits or feels different from the genuine one is the warning sign.

---

## Video 69 — Power: UPS & Generators and HVAC
- Duration: 08:28 schedule (lesson page settles at 08:27; the 08:28 label appeared only transiently before player metadata loaded on one reload)
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/b939431d-3782-483e-80f1-b931b06ba11f
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Infrastructure Support Systems 00:22; Power 00:42; Uninterruptible Powers Supply (UPS) 00:58 [chapter title spelled "Powers" on the page]; Generators 02:10; Power Outages 04:52; Power Degradation 05:10; Electrical Noise 05:42; Heating Ventilation & Air Conditioning (HVAC) 05:57
- Transcript: 00:18–08:13 (last block 07:51); 18 blocks / 149 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): Infrastructure support = clean, consistent power, network and HVAC. A UPS is essentially a large battery with two roles: instant short-term power when mains fail (power already flows through it) and power conditioning (cleaning up "dirty power" — wrong amplitude/frequency, spikes). Generators (large diesel + alternator) can power a whole facility but take roughly 20–60 seconds to spin up — exactly the gap the UPS bridges. They need constant maintenance (monthly runs, yearly load tests — the instructor explicitly says the exact numbers are NOT exam material, but the need for maintenance is), enough on-site fuel for the expected outage, and fuel-delivery contracts that land before the on-site supply runs out — with the caveat that government requisition for critical services overrode private fuel contracts in a Texas storm example. Power vocabulary to know: blackout = complete loss, long period; fault = complete loss, very short; brownout = prolonged low voltage, often deliberate by the utility to avert a blackout; sag/dip = brief low voltage; surge = brief excess voltage. Electrical noise = EMI/RFI on the line, countered by cable shielding and twisted pair. HVAC delivers air at the right temperature, humidity and cleanliness: too dry → static → shorts; too humid → condensation; unfiltered dust clogs heat sinks and overheats servers. Exact temperature/humidity ranges are explicitly NOT exam material. Final term: positive pressurization — keep the data centre slightly above ambient pressure so clean air pushes out through doors/cracks instead of contaminants leaking in.

---

## Video 70 — Power Degradation
- Duration: 03:21
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/a4c1b935-f2cc-49b7-8820-b734692d913d
- Chapters: Power Outages & Degradation 00:00; No Power 00:47; Not enough power 01:14; Too much power 02:22
- Transcript: 00:00–03:09; 9 blocks / 58 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): This video gives the precise short/long definitions that refine video 69's looser usage — flag the difference. Power problems sit on two axes: duration (short = a few milliseconds; long = seconds to days) and level (none / too little / too much), producing six terms the instructor explicitly says to memorise. No power: short = fault, long = blackout. Too little power: short = sag (also called a dip), long = brownout. Too much power: short = spike, long = surge. Reference voltage is 110 V (North American range 110–120 V); illustrations: ~80 V is low, a lightning-driven 500–1,000 V is a spike, ~200 V sustained all day is a surge. Brownouts are usually a deliberate utility move on high-demand days to avoid a full blackout — damaging to some equipment, but better than a blackout. Note this conflicts slightly with video 69, where "surge" was described as brief; this video's table (spike = short excess, surge = long excess) is the memorise-this version.

## Video 71 — Ideal Temperature & Humidity Ranges
- Duration: 03:41
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/85df9303-177f-4b34-a535-6db83e3e4569
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Ideal Temperature 02:14; Ideal Humidity 03:17
- Transcript: 00:07–03:27; 13 blocks / 60 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): NOTE — this corrects video 69, where the instructor said the exact ranges were not exam material; here he says the opposite for the ranges themselves: memorise the ranges, not the ASHRAE name expansion. The two numbers to know: ideal temperature 18–27 °C (64.4–80.6 °F) and ideal relative humidity 40–60%, both from ASHRAE. Reasoning recaps video 69: too hot → overheating; too humid → condensation/water in equipment; too dry → static damage. (Panel quirk: Fahrenheit is repeatedly rendered as "freedom height," apparently an instructor joke — do not take it literally.)

## Video 72 — Fire Detection
- Duration: 04:52
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/8452d8a5-2384-48c6-8060-479a98dfe23a
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Dealing with the Risk of Fire 00:25; Fire Detectors 01:36
- Transcript: 00:16–04:05; 11 blocks / 85 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): Fire risk is handled with prevention + detection + correction together. Best prevention is keeping combustibles out of the facility — e.g. unbox servers at the loading dock and leave the cardboard there. If fire occurs, detect as early as possible; suppression is the next video. Detector families, latest to earliest detection: flame detectors (cameras watching for IR/UV flame light — by the time flames are visible, detection is late); heat detectors (sensors watching for rapid temperature rise — fire already fairly advanced); smoke detectors (earliest of the three). Smoke-detector types: ionization (radioactive source ionises air between two plates; smoke disrupts the ion flow), photoelectric (off-angle light source; smoke reflects light into the sensor), dual-sensor (both combined; most common in homes/businesses), and VESDA — Very Early Smoke Detection Apparatus — the most sensitive and earliest of all, but expensive, so mainly data centres and museums. Exam tip flagged by the instructor: earliest fire detection = smoke detectors, and best smoke detector = VESDA.

---

## Video 73 — Fire Suppression
- Duration: 08:54
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/15f6d010-97f2-4799-94d2-7975de916dc9
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; Water Based Fire Suppression Systems 00:30; Gas Based Fire Suppression Systems 04:24; Fire Extinguishers 06:40
- Transcript: 00:14–08:39; 20 blocks / 158 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): Suppression is the corrective leg of the fire control set. Water systems are cheapest and simplest but wrong where water damage is itself costly (data centres, electrical equipment). Four water variants: wet pipe (pipes always full of pressurised water; cheapest, but leaks are inevitable and it cannot be used below 0 °C — freezing bursts pipes); dry pipe (pipes hold pressurised air/nitrogen instead of water; usually paired with pre-action); pre-action (a detection trigger must fire first, then water floods the dry pipes — a two-step that guards against accidental discharge); deluge (every head is open, so on alarm all heads discharge at once — for places like fireworks/TNT factories, not offices). Ordinary sprinkler heads open individually via a heat-sensitive glass vial. Gas systems either displace oxygen (fire triangle: heat, oxygen, fuel) or interrupt the combustion chemistry; the four gases to know are Inergen (captioned "Energen"), Argonite, FM-200 and Aero-K — all safe for electrical/valuable equipment. Halon is banned (greenhouse gas) — the instructor explicitly says never pick Halon on the exam. Aero-K is singled out as best around people because it does not remove oxygen; oxygen-displacing gases can kill occupants. Extinguisher classes: A = common combustibles (water/foam/dry chemical); B = flammable liquids (gas/CO₂, foam, dry chemical); C = electrical — never water (gas/CO₂/dry chemical; CO₂ is non-corrosive); D = combustible metals (dry powder); K = commercial kitchens (wet chemicals — never water on a grease/oil fire).

## Video 74 — Information System Lifecycle
- Duration: 02:28
- Lesson: https://new.my.destcert.com/schedule/lesson/c2dbca20-4523-4c00-be69-1ac6dc4b9214
- Chapters: Introduction 00:00; 3.10 Manage the information system lifecycle 00:20; What is the Information System Lifecycle? 00:24
- Transcript: 00:19–01:59; 6 blocks / 32 phrase lines (stable across three loads; no alternate version)
- Content summary (not a verbatim reproduction): A deliberately short section (guide section 3.10). The information system lifecycle is a system's whole lifespan, conception to decommissioning; steps in order: stakeholder needs/requirements, requirements analysis, architectural design, development and implementation, integration, validation and verification (testing), deployment, ongoing maintenance, disposal. The point of the lesson is the mapping to Domain 8: the SDLC (initiation → requirements → architecture/design → development → testing → release) is the first part of the system lifecycle; add operation and disposal and you have the full system lifecycle. Integration is not listed separately in the SDLC view because it sits inside testing. Detail is deferred to Domain 8 by design. Confirmed: this is the LAST main lesson video in Domain 3 — after it the schedule shows only the physical-security assessment, flashcards, results/MindMap items and practice questions.
